Full Parity-Violating Trispectrum in Axion Inflation: Reduction to Low-D Integrals
Matthew A. Reinhard, Zachary Slepian, Jiamin Hou, & Alessandro Greco

Abstract
Recent measurements of the galaxy 4-Point Correlation Function (4PCF) have seemingly detected non-zero parity-odd modes at high significance. Since gravity, the primary driver of galaxy formation and evolution is parity-even, any parity violation, if genuine, is likely to have been produced by some new parity-violating mechanism in the early Universe. Here we investigate an inflationary model with a Chern-Simons interaction between an axion and a U ⁡ ( 1 ) U(1) gauge field, where the axion itself is the inflaton field. Evaluating the trispectrum (Fourier-space analogue of the 4PCF) of the primordial curvature perturbations is an involved calculation with very high-dimensional loop integrals. We demonstrate how to simplify these integrals and perform all angular integrations analytically by reducing the integrals to convolutions and exploiting the Convolution Theorem. This leaves us with low-dimensional radial integrals that are much more amenable to efficient numerical evaluation. This paper is the first in a series in which we will use these results to compute the full late-time 4PCF for axion inflation, thence enabling constraints from upcoming 3D spectroscopic surveys such as Dark Energy Spectroscopic Instrument (DESI), Euclid, or Roman.



1
Introduction

The first measurements of the Cosmic Microwave Background (CMB) anisotropies introduced two major problems for Big Bang cosmology
[
1
]
. The uniformity in temperature of the CMB was unexpected for regions that were thought to have never been in causal contact. This is known as the horizon problem. Additionally, the temperature fluctuation power spectrum of the CMB corresponds to a Universe which has zero spatial curvature. Why the curvature takes on this very special value (it would have been zero to 59 decimal places at the Planck time) is known as the flatness problem
[
2
]
.

These two problems may be resolved by assuming a period of exponential, superluminal expansion of the early Universe, known as inflation
[
2
]
. Inflation generally posits the existence of a scalar field, known as the inflaton, whose energy density dominates the primordial Universe, driving the expansion of space
[
3
]
. Cosmic inflation also gives a mechanism for the production of the small relative temperature fluctuations in the CMB
[
4
]
. Quantum fluctuations of the inflaton field produce primordial curvature perturbations. These pockets of spacetime curvature accumulate matter and radiation, the imprint of which can be seen in the CMB anisotropies. These over-dense regions were also the seeds from which galaxies formed. Therefore, inflationary models provide a direct link between the behavior of quantum fields in the early Universe and the distribution of galaxies at late times
[
5
]
.

The N-Point Correlation Functions (NPCF) are a statistical tool that has proven to be of great utility in cosmology and beyond
[
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
]
. The NPCF measures the correlation of some quantity at
N
different spatial locations. The Fourier Transform (FT) of the 2-Point Correlation Function is known as the power spectrum. CMB measurements have shown that the scalar power spectrum is nearly scale-invariant, matching the predictions of single field slow-roll inflation. Both the galaxy 2PCF and 3PCF have been used to make complementary measurements of Baryon Acoustic Oscillation features in the distribution of galaxies, allowing us to better constrain the expansion history of the Universe
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
]
.

The galaxy 4PCF measures the excess clustering of quartets of galaxies over and above that which a spatially random distribution would possess. The 4PCF is the simplest statistic sensitive to parity violation in the 3D distribution of matter
[
15
]
.
[
16
]
earlier pointed out that correlating two position-dependent power spectra also probes parity, and
[
17
]
proposed the use of the 4PCF of the CMB anisotropies. A 3D parity transformation is simply taking
(x,y,z)\to(-x,-y,-z)
, i.e. spatial inversion. Geometrically, it corresponds to a mirror reflection followed by a
180^{\circ}
rotation. Recently, fast algorithms have been developed to measure the 4PCF
[
18
,
19
]
. The search proposed by
[
15
]
was carried out using the 4PCF of Sloan Digital Sky Survey (SDSS) Baryon Oscillation Spectroscopic Survey (BOSS) galaxies, finding
7.1\sigma
evidence for parity violation in the CMASS sample of roughly
800,000
Luminous Red Galaxies (LRGs) at
0.43<z<0.7
and roughly
3\sigma
evidence in the LOWZ (280,000 galaxies) lower-redshift sample of LRGs (
0.16<z<0.36
)
[
20
]
; see also
[
21
]
. These measurements both used tetrahedra, formed of sets of four galaxies at a time, that had sides ranging up to 328 Mpc/
h
(§5.1.4 of
[
20
]
); hence, on cosmological scales, where baryonic physics should not be important due to simple causality arguments. In particular, if we assume only standard model interactions, effects in galaxy formation such as feedback, supernovae, jets, and accretion, e.g.
[
22
,
23
,
24
]
, cannot contribute to parity violation. These processes involve the electromagnetic force which is parity even.

Despite these intriguing measurements, questions remain regarding the robustness of the results. Improvements can be made through various approaches, including the development of new statistical methods
[
25
]
, the identification of distinctive features in parity-odd signals
[
26
]
, and the explicit fitting of theoretical models, which serves as the primary motivation for this paper.

On large scales, galaxy formation is primarily governed by gravity, which is
also parity even. Perturbation theory has been used to compute the gravitationally induced even-parity galaxy 4PCF
[
27
]
and trispectrum (FT of the 4PCF)
[
28
]
. Any parity violation in large-scale structure (LSS) on these scales most likely came from very early in the Universe, during inflation. This is the only period during which the energy scales involved and physical interactions present are not sufficiently well-constrained by the Standard Model that parity violation due to them is ruled out.

In this work, we focus on a parity-violating inflationary model with the interaction
\phi F\tilde{F}
between the axion field
\phi
and a
U(1)
gauge field (the analog of the photon).
F
is the analog of the electromagnetic tensor and
\tilde{F}
is its dual. We will refer to this model as “axion inflation”. The first model utilizing an axion-like field as the inflaton was known as natural inflation
[
29
]
, and several variations of this model have been studied since
[
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
34
,
35
,
36
,
37
]
.

For the model considered in this paper, the axion-gauge field interaction produces disparate amounts of positive and negative helicity gauge quanta. This essentially means one will have a preference for one handedness of the gauge quanta over the other—exactly what parity violation is. Since these gauge quanta have energy density, General Relativity implies that they then produce curvature perturbations; these latter seed the galaxies that we observe at late times. This model is of particular interest for interpreting the parity-odd trispectrum observed in the BOSS analysis, since it predicts that the largest parity-odd signal comes from an equilateral configuration of wave vectors
[
38
,
39
]
, also see
(
40
, e.g.)
. In contrast,
[
41
,
42
]
proposed various models for parity violation, but the largest parity-odd contribution in those models come from the collapsed or squeezed limits.

To produce a prediction for this late-time signal, one must first compute the primordial curvature trispectrum. One then would apply linear transfer functions, possibly non-linear evolution (which should not be significant on the scales mostly relevant for the BOSS result), redshift-space distortions
[
43
]
, and finally a galaxy biasing scheme
[
44
]
. Previous works have calculated the tree-level trispectrum for parity violating inflationary models, such as axion inflation
[
17
,
37
]
and modified gravity
[
45
,
46
]
.

However, in practice the calculation of the primordial curvature trispectrum is already greatly complicated by the fact that the parity-odd component is only present at the one-loop level (or higher), if statistical isotropy is preserved. The presence of loop integrals on each of the internal momenta lead to a 16-dimensional integral.
[
39
]
was the first to compute the one-loop correction to the trispectrum for axion inflation, the model considered in this work, and
[
40
]
calculated the trispectrum for a similar inflationary model, but with the axion as a spectator field. These works numerically evaluated the loop integrals by brute force for a few different configurations of the four external wave-vectors; however, the bulk of the parameter space remains unexplored due to this approach’s numerical cost.

In this paper, we present an analytic method to accelerate dramatically the numerical computation of the primordial trispectrum for axion inflation. This acceleration is achieved by separating the angular and radial components of the trispectrum. We analytically evaluate the angular integrals and perform a change of variables on the radial integrals so as to facilitate efficient numerical computation.

In §
2
we present background material, including the equation of motion for the gauge field. In §
3
, we present the equations that govern the evolution of the inflaton perturbations. We discuss how to compute the primordial trispectrum in §
4
and present the operator ordering for each of the contributing pieces. In §
5
we demonstrate how the different ways of contracting the operator orderings of §
4
lead to only four independent kinds of diagrams. Furthermore, we decompose the trispectrum into four pieces, one for each of these diagram types. Each piece contains integrals over conformal time and the loop momenta. In §
6
we show that these integrals are convolutions, and make use of the Convolution Theorem to turn the problem into one of computing FTs. We demonstrate how to analytically compute the angular parts of these FTs, leaving us with only radial integrals. We use a change of variables to further reduce the dimensionality of these radial integrals in §
7
. In §
8
, we show how to put the (greatly simplified) integrals back into our four pieces of the trispectrum, giving us a complete expression for the primordial trispectrum. Additionally, we provide a table with a description of all the important ingredients necessary for constructing the trispectrum, as well as their location and mass dimension. In §
9
, we outline how efficient numerical integration of the low-dimensional radial integrals might proceed. §
10
concludes.

We include several appendices to clarify mathematical steps and list results that are omitted in the main part of this paper. Appendix
A
includes the multi-argument delta function expansions that we use extensively in §
5
. In Appendix
B
, we list all the convolution integrals from §
6
as well as the form they take after applying the Convolution Theorem. In Appendix
C
, we demonstrate how to perform the angular integration for the Fourier integrals listed in Appendix
B
. Throughout this work, we use an approximation for the gauge field mode functions given in Eq. (
13
). This is a good approximation only in a certain region of the integration space. However, our method for reducing the dimensionality of the loop integrals requires that we integrate over the whole space. In Appendix
D
, we verify numerically that extending the integration range (outside of the region of good approximation) for integrals containing these mode functions does not significantly change the result. In §
5
we state that the trispectrum cross terms are parity-even; Appendix
E
provides a proof of this claim.

2
U
(1) Axion Inflation

We consider an inflationary model with an axion field
\phi
coupled to a
U(1)
gauge field. Here, the axion
itself
is the inflaton, driving the accelerated expansion of space. During inflation, the axion’s potential energy will thus dominate the energy density, as is standard in the single-field “slow-roll” picture
[
47
]
. This inflationary model has been extensively studied in
[
33
,
48
,
39
]
. For a pedagogical review see
[
49
]
. For our presentation of the setup here, we follow closely the methods and notation of
(
50
)
.

The action
\mathcal{S}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\int\differential[]x\sqrt{-g}\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}M^{2}_{\rm p}R-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\phi\,\partial^{\mu}\phi-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}\,F^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{4\Lambda}\phi\,\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}\,F_{\mu\nu}-V(\phi)\Bigg]

(1)

where
g
is the determinant of the metric,
M_{\rm p}
is the reduced Planck mass,
R
is the Ricci scalar,
\partial_{\mu}
is a partial derivative with respect to the
\mu^{th}
dimension, and
\mu\in\{0,1,2,3\}
runs over time and space indices.
F_{\mu\nu}\equiv\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}
is the analog of the Faraday tensor for this field, with
A_{\mu}
the associated
U(1)
gauge field.
\Lambda^{-1}
is the coupling constant, which controls the strength of the interaction.
V(\phi)
is the inflaton potential. Tilde denotes the dual, and we have

\displaystyle\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}\equiv\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}F_{\rho\sigma}/2

(2)

where
\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
is the Levi-Civita tensor,

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\eta^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}.

(3)

\eta^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}

is
+1
for even permutations of its indices,
-1
for odd permutations, and zero if any indices are repeated.

We assume the potential is sufficiently flat to allow for a slowly rolling inflaton field. The (potential) slow-roll parameters are defined as

\displaystyle\epsilon\equiv\frac{M^{2}_{\rm p}}{2}\bigg(\frac{V^{\prime}(\phi)}{V(\phi)}\bigg)^{2},\hskip 14.22636pt\eta\equiv M^{2}_{\rm p}\bigg(\frac{V^{\prime\prime}(\phi)}{V(\phi)}\bigg).

(4)

We will assume that
\epsilon\ll 1
and
|\eta|\ll 1
.

During inflation, the rapid expansion of space very quickly flattens any initial curvature. Thus we assume a flat, homogeneous, and isotropic spacetime geometry. This geometry is described by the Friedmann-Lemaitre-Robertson-Walker metric:

\displaystyle ds^{2}=-dt^{2}+a^{2}(t)\,\differential\mathbf{x}\dotproduct\differential\mathbf{x}=a^{2}(\tau)[-\differential\tau^{2}+\differential\mathbf{x}\dotproduct\differential\mathbf{x}].

(5)

ds^{2}
is the line element, and
we have set the speed of light
c=1
here and do so throughout this work.
t
is the cosmic time;
a(t)
is the scale factor describing the dimensionless size of the Universe, and is conventionally set to unity at present. The dot product
\differential\mathbf{x}\dotproduct\differential\mathbf{x}
is Euclidean and
\differential\mathbf{x}
is a spatial 3-vector. The conformal time
\tau
is related to cosmic time
t
by
\differential t=a\,\differential\tau
.

We have ignored metric perturbations as these would simply add subleading terms to the equations of motion of the inflaton and gauge field. Further details about the inclusion of metric perturbations can be found in
[
50
]
. In this section, we are interested in the production of gauge quanta due to the rolling of the homogeneous inflaton background, thus we will also ignore inflaton perturbations. The inflaton perturbations will be reintroduced in §
3
.

For the gauge field
A_{\mu}
, we choose the Coulomb gauge; this means that
\divergence\mathbf{A}=0
. We also set the time component of
A_{\mu}
to be zero, i.e.
A_{0}=0
. This choice enables us to use the 3-vector
\mathbf{A}
in what follows. We further assume that the gauge field preserves statistical isotropy, i.e. it has a vanishing vacuum expectation value. We thus have the gauge field equation of motion as:

\mathbf{A}^{\prime\prime}-\laplacian\mathbf{A}-\frac{\bar{\phi}^{\prime}(\tau)}{\Lambda}\gradient\crossproduct\mathbf{A}=0,

(6)

where prime denotes a partial derivative with respect to the conformal time, i.e.
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}=\partial{\mathbf{A}}/\partial{\tau}
.

We may now define fields
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{B}
in analogy with the more familiar electric and magnetic fields. We have

\mathbf{E}=-{a(\tau)^{-2}}\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\hskip 19.91692pt\mathbf{B}={a(\tau)^{-2}}\,\curl\mathbf{A}.

(7)

We write the gauge field
\mathbf{A}
(evaluated at conformal time
\tau
and 3D position
\mathbf{x}
) as an inverse FT:

\mathbf{A}(\tau,\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\lambda=\pm}\int\frac{\differential[]\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\left[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{\lambda}(\hat{\mathbf{k}})a_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k})A_{\lambda}(\tau,k)e^{-i\mathbf{k}\dotproduct\mathbf{x}}+\text{h.c.}\right]

(8)

The wave-vector dependence of the Fourier-space field is decomposed into positive and negative helicity states
\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{\lambda}\equiv(\hat{\mathbf{\theta}}+i\lambda\hat{\mathbf{\phi}})/\sqrt{2}
, hence the sum over
\lambda=\pm
in front of the integral; h.c. stands for “Hermitian conjugate” of the previous term. The helicity eigenstates
\epsilon_{\lambda}
are also known as circular polarization vectors and satisfy the relations:

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}\cdot\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{\pm}(\hat{\mathbf{k}})=0,\hskip 5.69054pt\mathbf{k}\times\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{\pm}(\hat{\mathbf{k}})=\mp ik\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{\pm}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}),\hskip 5.69054pt\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{\pm}(-\hat{\mathbf{k}})=\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{\pm}^{*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}),

(9)

with normalization
\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{\lambda}^{*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\hat{\mathbf{k}})=\delta^{\rm K}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}
, where
∗
denotes complex conjugate and
\delta^{\rm K}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}
is the Kronecker Delta, unity when its subscripts are equal and zero otherwise.

In Eq. (
8
),
a_{\lambda}
is the gauge field annihilation operator; its Hermitian conjugate, which we denote
a_{\lambda}^{{\dagger}}
, is the gauge field creation operator. These operators obey the commutation relations

\commutator{a_\lambda(\vb k)}{a_{\lambda'}^\dag(\vb k')}=(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{\rm K}_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}\,\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}),\hskip 5.69054pt\commutator{a_\lambda(\vb k)}{a_{\lambda'}(\vb k')}=0,

(10)

with
\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}
the 3D Dirac delta function. In quantum mechanics one promotes the position and momentum of a particle to operators, and then imposes the canonical commutation relations on these operators. In quantum field theory, one replaces classical fields with operators and then canonically quantizes these operators. The commutation relations for the creation and annihilation operators given in Eq. (
10
) ensure that our fields have the correct canonical commutation relations
[
51
]
.

Here and throughout, our FT convention is that an inverse FT has a negative
i
in the exponential and is normalized as
\differential[]\mathbf{k}/(2\pi)^{3}
; a forward FT has no factors of
\pi
and a positive sign in the exponential. We note that both the sign in the exponential and the normalization differ from those used in
(
50
)
, which has a positive sign for an inverse FT and normalizes forward and inverse FTs symmetrically, with factors of
(2\pi)^{-3/2}
each.

Using the Fourier-space representation of the gauge field
{\bf A}
, Eq. (
8
), in the vectorial equation of motion for it, Eq. (
6
), we obtain the equation of motion for the amplitudes of the plus and minus modes of
{\bf A}
as

\displaystyle A_{\pm}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\tau,k)+k^{2}A_{\pm}(\tau,k)\mp\frac{2k\xi}{\tau}A_{\pm}(\tau,k)=0,

(11)

where

\xi\equiv\frac{\dot{\bar{\phi}}}{2\Lambda H(\tau)}.

\bar{\phi}
is the background value of the inflaton field and is spatially constant. We recall from the action, Eq. (
1
), that
\Lambda^{-1}
is the axion-gauge field coupling, and
H(\tau)
is the Hubble parameter. We have used the fact that
\tau\approx-1/(a(\tau)H(\tau))
for
\epsilon\ll 1
. During slow-roll inflation,
\xi
is approximately constant.

We note that at early times (
\tau\to-\infty
) the last term in Eq. (
11
), which is the only symmetry-breaking term, is negligible, and so both
A_{+}
and
A_{-}
will obey the equation of a simple harmonic oscillator, and gauge quanta of either helicity are equally produced. As inflation progresses, one of these modes is enhanced. The frequency of this oscillator is

\displaystyle\omega_{\pm}=\sqrt{k^{2}\mp 2k\xi/\tau}.

(12)

By analogy to the standard classical simple harmonic oscillator, we may regard the second term in the square-root above,
\mp 2k\xi/\tau
, as the square of a time-dependent mass. We assume
\dot{\bar{\phi}}>0
so that the
A_{-}
mode has an imaginary frequency (also called a tachyonic instability) for
-k\tau<2\xi
, leading to exponential production of modes with wave-numbers satisfying this condition. In the interval
(8\xi)^{-1}\lesssim-k\tau\lesssim 2\xi
, the solution to Eq. (
11
) is well-approximated by
[
48
]
:

\displaystyle A_{-}(\tau,k)\approx\frac{1}{\sqrt{2k}}\left(\frac{-k\tau}{2\xi}\right)^{1/4}e^{\pi\xi-2\sqrt{-2\xi k\tau}}

(13)

Almost all of the gauge field fluctuations produced are in this region of
k\tau
. From this point on, we thus ignore the
A_{+}
modes since they experience no tachyonic instability and so their production is negligible.

A parity transformation takes a positive helicity mode function to a negative helicity one and vice versa.
The imbalance in production of the two helicity modes is thus a violation of parity symmetry, which has arisen due to the
\phi\tilde{F}F
interaction between the axion and the gauge field
.

Eq. (
6
) describes the production of gauge quanta. These particles may back-react, altering the time evolution of the inflaton background. The backreaction must be controlled so as not to spoil inflation. A detailed analysis of this backreaction in
(
50
)
, finds that slow-roll inflation is preserved if two conditions are satisfied:

\displaystyle\frac{H(\tau)^{2}}{2\pi|\dot{\bar{\phi}}|}\ll 13\,\xi^{3/2}\,e^{-\pi\xi},

(14)

\displaystyle\frac{H(\tau)}{M_{\rm p}}\ll 146\,\xi^{3/2}\,e^{-\pi\xi}.

Towards the end of inflation there may be a significant contribution to the trispectrum (possibly both even and odd) due to the backreaction
[
52
,
53
]
. In this work, we assume that this effect is negligible. The stronger the interaction between the axion and the gauge field (
\Lambda^{-1}
) the earlier during inflation that backreaction will become prevalent. According to
figueroa2023
, as inflation progresses and the backreaction becomes significant, the production of
A_{+}
modes will increase, reducing the imbalance of positive and negative helicity modes, and thus suppressing parity violation towards the end of inflation. Our result for the trispectrum will therefore be most accurate for scales larger than some cutoff corresponding to the transition into the strong backreaction regime. Thus when searching for evidence of axion inflation in the parity-odd galaxy 4PCF, it may be useful to consider only those larger scales which exited the horizon during the earlier part of inflation, when this suppression is not relevant.

The inverse decay of the gauge quanta into inflaton fluctuations will be the source of parity violation in the primordial trispectrum. We study these inverse decay fluctuations in §
3
.

3
Production of Inflaton Fluctuations

In addition to the backreaction, the gauge quanta can also source small fluctuations in the inflaton field through an inverse decay process. Here, we study these inverse decay fluctuations. We consider small fluctuations
\delta\phi
in the inflaton field,
\phi
, around its spatially uniform (but time-dependent) background
\bar{\phi}
. We thus write

\displaystyle\phi(\tau,\mathbf{x})=\bar{\phi}(\tau)+\delta\phi(\tau,\mathbf{x}).

(15)

From the action, Eq. (
1
), we have the inflaton field’s equation of motion as

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime\prime}+2\mathcal{H}(\tau)\phi^{\prime}-\laplacian\phi+a(\tau)^{2}\derivative{V}{\phi}=\frac{a(\tau)^{2}}{\Lambda}\,\mathbf{E}\dotproduct\mathbf{B}

(16)

where
\mathcal{H}(\tau)=d\ln a/d\tau
is the conformal Hubble parameter and
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{B}
are defined in Eq. (
7
). We recall that
V
is the inflationary potential. We have again ignored metric perturbations since their inclusion introduces only subleading terms in Eq. (
16
). We note that the gauge field
\mathbf{A}
arises in the source term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
16
) through
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{B}
, leading to production of inflaton particles via inverse decays.

Substituting Eq. (
15
) into Eq. (
16
), we obtain the equation of motion for the perturbations as

\delta\phi^{\prime\prime}+2\mathcal{H}(\tau)\delta\phi^{\prime}-\laplacian\delta\phi+a(\tau)^{2}\derivative[2]{V}{\bar\phi}\delta\phi=\frac{a(\tau)^{2}}{\Lambda}\left(\mathbf{E}\dotproduct\mathbf{B}-\expectationvalue{\vb E \vdot\vb B}\right),

(17)

where
\expectationvalue{\vb E \vdot\vb B}
denotes a spatial average of the dot product of the electric and magnetic field vectors.
We may now further separate the fluctuations
\delta\phi
in the inflaton field into:

i)
vacuum fluctuations and

ii)
fluctuations produced by inverse decay of the gauge quanta.

These correspond respectively to the
i)
homogeneous and
ii)
particular solutions of the differential equation (
17
). With this in mind, we write the full solution as

\delta\phi(\tau,\mathbf{x})=\delta\phi^{\text{vac}}(\tau,\mathbf{x})+\delta\phi^{\text{inv}}(\tau,\mathbf{x}).

(18)

These two types of fluctuations are statistically independent of each other because they arise via two independent mechanisms.

We now write the fluctuations as an inverse FT:

\displaystyle\delta\phi(\tau,\mathbf{x})=\int\frac{\differential[]\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{Q(\tau,\mathbf{k})}{a(\tau)}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\dotproduct\mathbf{x}};

(19)

Q
is conventionally called the “mode function”. Now inserting this decomposition into the perturbations’ equation of motion, Eq. (
17
), we obtain

\left[\partialderivative[2]{\tau}+k^{2}+a(\tau)^{2}\derivative[2]{V}{\bar\phi}-\frac{a(\tau)^{\prime\prime}}{a(\tau)}\right]Q(\tau,\mathbf{k})=J(\tau,\mathbf{k}),

(20)

where

J(\tau,\mathbf{k})\equiv\frac{a(\tau)^{3}}{\Lambda}\int\differential[]\mathbf{x}\;\mathbf{E}\dotproduct\mathbf{B}\,e^{i\mathbf{k}\dotproduct\mathbf{x}}.

(21)

We recall that
a(\tau)
is the scale factor,
\Lambda^{-1}
is the axion-gauge field coupling, and
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{B}
are defined in Eq. (
7
) in terms of the gauge field
\mathbf{A}
. We note that the second term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
17
) has been spatially averaged and is thus dependent only on time. Thus its spatial FT gives a Dirac delta function, meaning that this term only affects modes at zero wave-number; we may therefore neglect it.

We may now separate the mode function
Q
into its vacuum and inverse decay components, as

Q(\tau,\mathbf{k})=Q^{\text{vac}}(\tau,\mathbf{k})+Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau,\mathbf{k}).

(22)

We further decompose the vacuum contribution as

Q^{\text{vac}}(\tau,\mathbf{k})=b(\mathbf{k})\varphi(\tau,k)+b^{\dagger}(-\mathbf{k})\varphi^{*}(\tau,k),

(23)

where
b(\mathbf{k})
and
b^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})
are the
inflaton
annihilation and creation operators, respectively (as distinct from the
U(1)
gauge quanta annihilation and creation operators
a
and
a^{\dagger}
). These operators obey the commutation relations

\displaystyle\commutator{b(\vb k)}{b^\dag(\vb k')}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}),\hskip 5.69054pt\commutator{b(\vb k)}{b(\vb k')}=0,

(24)

\displaystyle\commutator{b(\vb k)}{a_\lambda^\dag(\vb k')}

\displaystyle=\commutator{b(\vb k)}{a_\lambda(\vb k')}=0.

\varphi(\tau,k)
is the homogeneous solution of Eq. (
20
):

\varphi(\tau,k)=i\frac{\sqrt{-\pi\tau}}{2}\,H_{\nu}^{(1)}(-k\tau),\qquad\nu\approx\frac{3}{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon,\eta),

(25)

where we have assumed a Bunch-Davies vacuum and
H_{\nu}^{(1)}
is a Hankel function of the first kind.
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon,\eta)
denotes terms which are first (or higher) order in
\epsilon
and
\eta
. The factor of
i
is an arbitrary phase which was chosen so that
\varphi(\tau,k)
is real in the limit as
-k\tau\to 0
. We note that
\varphi
depends only on the magnitude of the wave vector
\mathbf{k}
. From these vacuum modes
\varphi
, we construct the retarded Green’s function as:

G(\tau,\tau^{\prime},k)=i\Theta(\tau-\tau^{\prime})\left[\varphi(\tau,k)\varphi^{*}(\tau^{\prime},k)-\varphi^{*}(\tau,k)\varphi(\tau^{\prime},k)\right].

(26)

Here

\Theta(\tau-\tau^{\prime})\equiv\begin{cases}1&\tau^{\prime}\leq\tau\\
0&\tau^{\prime}>\tau\end{cases}

is the Heaviside step function. This function enforces causality: a mode function at time
\tau
is influenced only by the past behavior of the source, i.e. at times
\tau^{\prime}\leq\tau
.

With this in hand, we now find the particular solution to Eq. (
20
) as

\displaystyle Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau,\mathbf{k})=\int_{-\infty}^{\tau}\differential\tau^{\prime}\,G(\tau,\tau^{\prime},k)\,J(\tau^{\prime},\mathbf{k}),

(27)

where
J(\tau,\mathbf{k})
was defined in Eq. (
21
). Substituting Eqs. (
7
) and (
8
) into Eq. (
21
), we find

\displaystyle J(\tau,\mathbf{k})=-\frac{1}{\Lambda a(\tau)}\int\frac{\differential[]\mathbf{q}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,q\,[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})]

(28)

\displaystyle\times\bigg\{a_{-}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})A_{-}^{\prime}(\tau,|\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}|)a_{-}(\mathbf{q})A_{-}(\tau,q)+a_{-}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})A_{-}^{\prime}(\tau,|\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}|)a_{-}^{\dagger}(-\mathbf{q})A_{-}^{*}(\tau,q)

\displaystyle+a_{-}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{k})A_{-}^{\prime*}(\tau,|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{k}|)a_{-}(\mathbf{q})A_{-}(\tau,q)+a_{-}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{k})A_{-}^{\prime*}(\tau,|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{k}|)a_{-}^{\dagger}(-\mathbf{q})A_{-}^{*}(\tau,q)\bigg\}.

We recall that
\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{k}})
is the negative-helicity polarization vector defined before Eq. (
9
), the primes on the mode functions
A_{-}
(which themselves are defined in Eq. (
8
)) are derivatives with respect to conformal time
\tau
, and lowercase
a_{-}
is the gauge field annihilation operator, with
a_{-}^{\dagger}
the creation operator. Since the production of positive helicity modes is negligible, we have not included them in the source term
J(\tau,\mathbf{k})
.

4
The Primordial Trispectrum

We will now calculate the FT of the 4PCF of the primordial curvature perturbations; this is known as the primordial trispectrum. There are several methods used to study cosmological correlation functions
[
54
,
55
,
56
,
38
]
. In this paper, we effectively employ the in-in formalism
[
57
,
58
,
51
,
59
]
. In particular, we follow the method of
[
50
]
, which is equivalent to the in-in formalism for the case of a real mode function. The error associated with using the real mode approximation in the range
1.55\leq\xi\leq 2.4
is below 1% as found in
[
39
]
.
[
50
]
found that inverse decay effects are negligible for
\xi\lesssim 1
and rule out
\xi\gtrsim 2.65
using data from the Cosmic Background Explorer. Thus the real part of the mode function dominates for most of the allowed region of the parameter space. For this reason, we consider only the real part of the gauge field mode functions throughout this work.

We choose the spatially flat gauge, for which the curvature perturbation on uniform density hyper-surfaces is
\zeta(\tau,\mathbf{x})=-H(\tau)\delta\phi(\tau,\mathbf{x})/\dot{\phi}(\tau)
. We now write the curvature perturbation as an inverse FT:

\displaystyle\zeta(\tau,\mathbf{x})=\int\frac{\differential^{3}\mathbf{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\zeta(\tau,\mathbf{k})\,e^{-i\mathbf{k}\dotproduct\mathbf{x}}.

(29)

Using Eq. (
19
), we can rewrite the FT of the curvature perturbation as

\displaystyle\zeta(\tau,\mathbf{k})=-Q(\tau,\mathbf{k})H(\tau)/(a(\tau)\dot{\phi}(\tau)).

(30)

The trispectrum evaluated at time
\tau
is

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\zeta(\tau, \vb k_1) \zeta(\tau, \vb k_2) \zeta(\tau, \vb k_3) \zeta(\tau, \vb k_4)}=\frac{H(\tau)^{4}}{\dot{\phi}(\tau)^{4}\hskip 1.42262pta(\tau)^{4}}\bigg[

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{Q^{\text{vac}}(\tau, \vb k_1) Q^{\text{vac}}(\tau, \vb k_2) Q^{\text{vac}}(\tau, \vb k_3) Q^{\text{vac}}(\tau, \vb k_4)}

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt+\expectationvalue{Q^{\text{vac}}(\tau, \vb k_1) Q^{\text{vac}}(\tau, \vb k_2) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_3) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_4)}+\text{symm}.

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt+\expectationvalue{Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_1) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_2) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_3) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_4)}\bigg],

(31)

where the
<\cdots>
represents a vacuum expectation value of the included operators and symm. denotes a symmetrization of the previous term over all possible arguments. We note that any terms including an odd number of
Q^{\text{vac}}
operators must also have an odd number of inflaton creation and annihilation operators and therefore their vacuum expectation value must vanish since these operators commute with the gauge field creation and annihilation operators.

The trispectrum contains both parity-even and parity-odd terms. Although parity symmetry is highly violated in the gauge field, where one of the modes grows exponentially while the other one gets largely suppressed,
the parity-odd contribution has been found to be subdominant compared to the parity-even part of the trispectrum
[
39
,
40
]
. The only term in Eq. (
31
) containing parity-odd modes is the last one, which contains four inverse decay mode functions.
1
1

1

As explained in §
2
, parity-violation arises from the interaction vertex
\phi\tilde{F}F
; this vertex appears only at one loop and beyond. The expectation values that contain two vacuum and two inverse decay mode functions are in fact parity-even, and we have proven this fact in Appendix
E
.
To obtain the parity-odd trispectrum we thus need only to calculate this last term:

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_1) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_2) \nonumber Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_3) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_4)}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{1}\int_{-\infty}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{3}\int_{-\infty}^{\tau}\differential\tau_{4}\;G(\tau,\tau_{1},k_{1})\,G(\tau,\tau_{2},k_{2})\,G(\tau,\tau_{3},k_{3})\,G(\tau,\tau_{4},k_{4})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\expectationvalue{J(\tau_1, \vb k_2)\, J(\tau_2, \vb k_2) \,J(\tau_3, \vb k_3) \, J(\tau_4, \vb k_4)}.

(32)

We will call this term the inverse decay trispectrum. Although this term contains both parity-odd and even contributions, we can always project the result onto the isotropic basis functions of
[
60
]
to single out the parity-odd modes.

We evaluate the trispectrum for modes well outside of the horizon; thus we take the limit
-k\tau\rightarrow 0
(i.e. very small
k
). We may therefore employ an approximation for
\varphi(\tau,k)
but not for
\varphi(\tau^{\prime},k)
. The Green’s function of Eq. (
26
) then becomes

\displaystyle G(\tau,\tau^{\prime},k)

\displaystyle=i\Theta(\tau-\tau^{\prime})\frac{a(\tau)H(\tau)}{\sqrt{2k^{3}}}(-k\tau)^{\frac{n_{s}-1}{2}}\big[\varphi^{*}(\tau^{\prime},k)-\varphi(\tau^{\prime},k)\big]

\displaystyle=a(\tau)H(\tau)\sqrt{\frac{-\pi\tau^{\prime}}{8k^{3}}}(-k\tau)^{\frac{n_{s}-1}{2}}\Theta(\tau-\tau^{\prime})\left[H_{3/2}^{(1)*}(-k\tau^{\prime})+H_{3/2}^{(1)}(-k\tau^{\prime})\right],

(33)

where we have used Eq. (
25
) as well as the approximation that

\displaystyle\varphi(\tau,k)\approx\frac{a(\tau)H(\tau)}{\sqrt{2k^{3}}}(-k\tau)^{\frac{n_{s}-1}{2}}

(34)

for
-k\tau\rightarrow 0
.

We now use

\displaystyle\real[H_{3/2}^{(1)}(x)]=J_{3/2}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{2x}{\pi}}\,j_{1}(x)

(35)

to rewrite the Green’s function as

\displaystyle G(\tau,\tau^{\prime},k)=\Theta(\tau-\tau^{\prime})\frac{a(\tau)H(\tau)\tau^{\prime}}{k}(-k\tau)^{\frac{n_{s}-1}{2}}j_{1}(-k\tau^{\prime}).

(36)

In the next section, we will focus on evaluating the expectation value of the product of the four source functions
J(\tau,\mathbf{k})
, which enters the integral in Eq. (
32
).
Each
J(\tau,\mathbf{k})
contains creation and annihilation operators. To compute the correlation function, we must consider all possible Wick contractions of these operators.

4.1
Counting Contractions

We will compute the inverse decay trispectrum at the one-loop level. We begin by
inserting Eq. (
28
) for
J
into the correlation function of the source terms in Eq. (
32
),

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{JJJJ}\equiv\expectationvalue{J(\tau_1, \vb k_2) J(\tau_2, \vb k_2) J(\tau_3, \vb k_3) J(\tau_4, \vb k_4)}.

(37)

4.1.1
Operator Orderings

We find that the non-vanishing pieces of the correlation function at the one-loop level contain creation and annihilation operators in the following orderings. We highlight that ordering does matter here as these are operators. We also note that, up to overall sign, the arguments in the list below do not change as we go from I through V (up to an overall minus sign).

\displaystyle\text{I}.\hskip 14.22636pt

\displaystyle a_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1})a_{-}(\mathbf{q}_{1})a_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2})a_{-}(\mathbf{q}_{2})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{q}_{3}-\mathbf{k}_{3})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(-\mathbf{q}_{3})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{q}_{4}-\mathbf{k}_{4})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(-\mathbf{q}_{4})

\displaystyle\text{II}.\hskip 14.22636pt

\displaystyle a_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1})a_{-}(\mathbf{q}_{1})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{q}_{2}-\mathbf{k}_{2})a_{-}(\mathbf{q}_{2})a_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(-\mathbf{q}_{3})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{q}_{4}-\mathbf{k}_{4})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(-\mathbf{q}_{4})

\displaystyle\text{III}.\hskip 14.22636pt

\displaystyle a_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1})a_{-}(\mathbf{q}_{1})a_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(-\mathbf{q}_{2})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{q}_{3}-\mathbf{k}_{3})a_{-}(\mathbf{q}_{3})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{q}_{4}-\mathbf{k}_{4})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(-\mathbf{q}_{4})

\displaystyle\text{IV}.\hskip 14.22636pt

\displaystyle a_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1})a_{-}(\mathbf{q}_{1})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{q}_{2}-\mathbf{k}_{2})a_{-}(\mathbf{q}_{2})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{q}_{3}-\mathbf{k}_{3})a_{-}(\mathbf{q}_{3})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{q}_{4}-\mathbf{k}_{4})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(-\mathbf{q}_{4})

\displaystyle\text{V}.\hskip 14.22636pt

\displaystyle a_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1})a_{-}(\mathbf{q}_{1})a_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(-\mathbf{q}_{2})a_{-}(\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(-\mathbf{q}_{3})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(\mathbf{q}_{4}-\mathbf{k}_{4})a_{-}^{{\dagger}}(-\mathbf{q}_{4})

(38)

Each term has a different ordering of creation and annihilation operators; we will term this the “operator ordering”.

4.1.2
Contractions Within Each Operator Ordering

Within each of the five operator orderings of Eq. (
38
) above, we must now consider all of the possible ways we can contract the creation and annihilation operators to form a 1-loop diagram. Figure
1
shows the four vertices corresponding to the four external momenta of the trispectrum, the internal lines of which can be connected to form a one-loop diagram. The connection of two internal lines corresponds to a contraction.

Now, to form a valid contraction of a single creation and single annihilation operator, the creation operator must be on the right-hand side of the pair. Each contraction of a creation and annihilation operator gives a Dirac delta function:

The contraction can be represented pictorially as connecting a pair of internal momentum lines in Figure
1
. We now give one example of a contraction combination for operator ordering I.

To represent this contraction combination pictorially one would connect all of the internal lines in Figure
1
. Thus we will often refer to a contraction combination, or the four Dirac delta functions that it yields, as a “diagram”. Specifically, in this paper, the word “diagram” will mean that all of the internal and external momenta are specified with numerical subscripts. According to Wick’s theorem, the correlation function of the
J
’s, Eq. (
37
), receives a contribution from all such diagrams.

We will denote the sum of all diagrams that have a given operator ordering, I, II, III, IV, or V, as
\Delta_{\text{I}}
,
\Delta_{\text{II}}
,
\Delta_{\text{III}}
,
\Delta_{\text{IV}}
, or
\Delta_{\text{V}}
.
We now have the full correlation function given in Eq. (
37
) as

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{JJJJ}=\frac{1}{\Lambda^{4}a^{4}(\tau)}\int\int\int\int\frac{\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{2}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{3}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{4}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,(\Delta_{\text{I}}+\Delta_{\text{II}}+\Delta_{\text{III}}+\Delta_{\text{IV}}+\Delta_{\text{V}})

\displaystyle\left[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}})\right]\left[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{2})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2}})\right]\left[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{3})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}})\right]\left[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}})\right]

\displaystyle\times q_{1}A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{1},|\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}|)A_{-}(\tau_{1},q_{1})\times q_{2}A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{2},|\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2}|)A_{-}(\tau_{2},q_{2})

\displaystyle\times q_{3}A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{3},|\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}|)A_{-}(\tau_{3},q_{3})\times q_{4}A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{4},|\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}|)A_{-}(\tau_{4},q_{4}),

(40)

and where

\displaystyle\Delta_{\text{I}}\equiv(2\pi)^{12}\Bigg\{\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{2}+\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{q}_{4})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2}+\mathbf{q}_{3})

\displaystyle\hskip 68.2866pt+\text{all other contraction combinations for operator ordering I.}\Bigg\}

(41)

corresponds to the sum of all diagrams with operator ordering I. The first term in Eq. (
41
) is given by the contraction combination below Eq. (
). The other contraction combinations within operator ordering I simply lead to different arrangements of the momenta in the arguments of the Dirac delta functions. The Dirac delta functions corresponding to the other diagrams in operator orderings I-V can be found in Figure
2
. We note that since we are considering only the real mode approximation for the gauge field mode functions, we are able to ignore the complex conjugates on these mode functions and pull the mode functions out as a common factor for the
J
’s.

For operator ordering I, there are sixteen contraction combinations, each representing a one-loop diagram. For operator orderings II through V, there are eight diagrams, as we show in Figure
2
. Adding up the number of diagrams for each of the five operator orderings in Eq. (
38
), we see that the correlation function of the
J
’s will be a sum of 48 different terms.

Figure 1:
The gray lines represent the external momenta
\vec{k}_{i}
. The internal lines (red and blue) of the four vertices can be connected in several ways to form a one-loop diagram. Each time we contract two operators, we can represent this pictorially by connecting internal lines from two different vertices. We will associate each blue line with a “simple momentum”
\vec{q}_{i}
, and each red line with a “compound momentum”
\vec{k}_{j}-\vec{q}_{j}
. For a given contraction, the momentum subscript with the larger integer will always correspond to a creation operator, and the momentum momentum subscript with the smaller integer will always correspond to an annihilation operator.

Figure 2:
This figure represents all one-loop diagrams produced by considering all possible contraction combinations of the creation and annihilation operators within each operator ordering. Each contraction is represented by a “flag” with two numbers. The color blue corresponds to an internal line with simple momentum
\mathbf{q}_{i}
while the color red corresponds to an internal line with compound momentum
\mathbf{k}_{i}-\mathbf{q}_{i}
. The subscript of the momentum is determined by the numbers in the flag, e.g.
represents connecting the internal lines with momenta
\mathbf{q}_{1}
and
\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}
. Within each operator ordering, each row of four flags shows us how to connect the internal lines in Figure
1
, thus giving a one-loop diagram.

Figure 3:
Here we have categorized all of the terms from Figure
2
by their connection structure. The meaning of the individual colored flags and the fact that each row of four flags corresponds to a one-loop diagram is described in the Figure
2
caption. The four connection structures are presented in Figure
4
. The significance of these connection structures is described in §
5
.

Figure 4:
The four connection structures are determined by the colors of the connected internal lines above (the external lines are grey). The color patterns correspond to the different ways one can contract the creation and annihilation operators with momenta
\mathbf{k}_{i}-\mathbf{q}_{i}
and
\mathbf{q}_{j}
(red-blue),
\mathbf{q}_{i}
and
\mathbf{q}_{j}
(blue-blue), and
\mathbf{k}_{i}-\mathbf{q}_{i}
and
\mathbf{k}_{j}-\mathbf{q}_{j}
(red-red). The chosen contraction combination determines the number of momentum vectors in the argument of the delta function. These four diagrams represent the four connection structures of §
5
.

Figure 5:
Here we show four of the forty-eight possible diagrams, one representing each of the four connection structures. The numbering of each diagram corresponds to its connection structure. The diagrams within each connection structure can be related by interchanging the subscripts on the momenta. Due to this fact, we consider only a single diagram for each of the four connection structures when computing the trispectrum in
§
5
. We call these four diagrams “Reference Diagrams”.

5
Classifying Diagrams into Four Connection Structures

The contractions outlined in §
4
give the whole correlation function of the
J
’s as a sum of 48 terms. Each term contains four Dirac delta functions and corresponds to one of the 48 diagrams. It is notable that each of these diagrams can be classified into one of just four types. We term these four types “connection structures”.
In Figure
3
, we have grouped the diagrams according to their connection structures. In Figure
4
we represent these connection structures pictorially.
2
2

2

We note that this is not the only way to compute the contractions. Alternatively, one can insert a Dirac delta function to enforce the symmetry at each vertex; this corresponds to momentum conservation at each vertex. However, in this paper, we will proceed with the first approach, since it will allow us to easily write down the contribution to the trispectrum for any of the 48 diagrams once we have calculated this result for a single diagram from each of the four connection structures.

Diagrams with the same connection structure are related to one another by interchanging the subscripts on the external momenta
\mathbf{k}_{i}
.
3
3

3

The subscripts of the loop momenta
\mathbf{q}_{i}
can be changed as well since these are integration variables.
However, for diagrams with different connection structures it is not possible to do such an interchange of the momenta.
In this sense, diagrams with different connection structures are completely independent of one another.

In this section, we will compute the contribution to the trispectrum for each connection structure. We may consider just a single diagram for each connection structure, since the final result for two diagrams with the same connection structure can be related by simply relabeling the subscripts of the external momenta.

We now perform the computations for four independent diagrams, one for each connection structure. We will call these “Reference Diagrams”; they are shown in Figure
5
. We have

\displaystyle(1)\hskip 14.22636pt

\displaystyle\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{2}+\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{4}+\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{3}+\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2})

\displaystyle(2)\hskip 14.22636pt

\displaystyle\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{2}+\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{3}+\mathbf{q}_{4})

\displaystyle(3)\hskip 14.22636pt

\displaystyle\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}+\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{q}_{3})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{2}+\mathbf{q}_{4})

\displaystyle(4)\hskip 14.22636pt

\displaystyle\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{4}+\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{2}+\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{q}_{3}).

In order to simplify the trispectrum computation, we will rewrite the Dirac delta functions as FTs of unity, and then use the plane wave expansion to separate the dependence on each argument. This Dirac delta function expansion is described in more detail in Appendix
A
.

We now determine the contribution to the trispectrum from each of the four connection structures.

5.1
First Connection Structure Example:
Reference Diagram 1

We begin by inserting the first term in Eq. (
41
), which corresponds to
Reference Diagram 1
in Figure
5
, into Eq. (
40
),

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{J(\tau_1, \vb k_1) J(\tau_2, \vb k_2) J(\tau_3, \vb k_3) J(\tau_4, \vb k_4)}^{(1)}\equiv

(42)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Lambda^{4}a^{4}(\tau)}\int\int\int\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{1}\ \differential[]\mathbf{q}_{2}\ \differential[]\mathbf{q}_{3}\ \differential[]\mathbf{q}_{4}\;q_{1}\;q_{2}\;q_{3}\;q_{4}\left[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}})\right]

\displaystyle\times\left[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{2})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2}})\right]\left[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{3})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}})\right]\left[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}})\right]\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{2}+\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4})

\displaystyle\times\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{q}_{4})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2}+\mathbf{q}_{3})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{1},|\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}|)A_{-}(\tau_{1},q_{1})

\displaystyle\times A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{2},|\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2}|)A_{-}(\tau_{2},q_{2})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{3},|\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}|)A_{-}(\tau_{3},q_{3})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{4},|\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}|)A_{-}(\tau_{4},q_{4}).

Upon using the expansion (
144

\displaystyle\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{2}+\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{q}_{4})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2}+\mathbf{q}_{3})

(43)

\displaystyle=8\sum_{\lambda_{2}\ell_{4}\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}\sum_{\mu_{2}m_{4}\mu^{\prime}_{4}}i^{\lambda_{2}+\ell_{4}+\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{2}\ell_{4}\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}^{\mu_{2}m_{4}\mu^{\prime}_{4}}\mathcal{R}_{\lambda_{2}\ell_{4}\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}(q_{2},k_{4},q_{4})Y_{\lambda_{2}}^{\mu_{2}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{2})Y_{\ell_{4}}^{m_{4}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{4})Y_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{4}*}(-\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})

\displaystyle\times 8\sum_{\lambda_{1}\ell_{3}\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}\sum_{\mu_{1}m_{3}\mu^{\prime}_{3}}i^{\lambda_{1}+\ell_{3}+\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{1}\ell_{3}\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}^{\mu_{1}m_{3}\mu^{\prime}_{3}}\mathcal{R}_{\lambda_{1}\ell_{3}\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}(q_{1},k_{3},q_{3})Y_{\lambda_{1}}^{\mu_{1}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})Y_{\ell_{3}}^{m_{3}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{3})Y_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{3}*}(-\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{3})

\displaystyle\times 8\sum_{\ell_{1}\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{4}}\sum_{m_{1}\mu^{\prime}_{1}\mu_{4}}i^{\ell_{1}+\lambda^{\prime}_{1}+\lambda_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{1}\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{4}}^{m_{1}\mu^{\prime}_{1}\mu_{4}}\mathcal{R}_{\ell_{1}\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{4}}(k_{1},q_{1},q_{4})Y_{\ell_{1}}^{m_{1}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{1})Y_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{1}*}(-\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})Y_{\lambda_{4}}^{\mu_{4}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})

\displaystyle\times 8\sum_{\ell_{2}\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{3}}\sum_{m_{2}\mu^{\prime}_{2}\mu_{3}}i^{\ell_{2}+\lambda^{\prime}_{2}+\lambda_{3}}\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{2}\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{3}}^{m_{2}\mu^{\prime}_{2}\mu_{3}}\mathcal{R}_{\ell_{2}\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{3}}(k_{2},q_{2},q_{3})Y_{\ell_{2}}^{m_{2}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{2})Y_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{2}*}(-\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{2})Y_{\lambda_{3}}^{\mu_{3}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{3}).

\mathcal{G}
is the Gaunt integral and
\mathcal{R}
is an overlap integral of spherical Bessel functions;
\mathcal{G}
and

are defined mathematically in Eqs. (

) and (

\ell_{i},\lambda_{i},\lambda^{\prime}_{i}
as the angular momenta corresponding to vectors
\mathbf{k}_{i},\mathbf{q}_{i}
, and
-\mathbf{q}_{i}
respectively. In these sums,
\ell_{i},\lambda_{i},\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
4
4

4

\mathbb{N}_{0}
is the set of natural numbers including zero.
and the
z
-components of the angular momenta
m_{i},\mu_{i},\mu^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Each
z

-\ell_{i}\leq m_{i}\leq\ell_{i}
.

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{J(\tau, \vb k_1) J(\tau, \vb k_2) J(\tau, \vb k_3) J(\tau, \vb k_4)}^{(1)}=

\displaystyle\frac{4{,}096}{\Lambda^{4}a^{4}(\tau)}\sum_{\mathbf{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{m}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda},\bm{\mu}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda}^{\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}\Bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{4}i^{\ell_{j}+\lambda_{j}+\lambda^{\prime}_{j}}Y_{\ell_{j}}^{m_{j}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{j})\Bigg]\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{2}\ell_{4}\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}^{\mu_{2}m_{4}\mu^{\prime}_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{1}\ell_{3}\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}^{\mu_{1}m_{3}\mu^{\prime}_{3}}\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{1}\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{4}}^{m_{1}\mu^{\prime}_{1}\mu_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{2}\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{3}}^{m_{2}\mu^{\prime}_{2}\mu_{3}}

\displaystyle\times\int\int\int\int\differential x_{1}\,\differential x_{2}\,\differential x_{3}\,\differential x_{4}\,\,x_{1}^{2}\,x_{2}^{2}\,x_{3}^{2}\,x_{4}^{2}\,\,j_{\ell_{4}}(k_{4}x_{1})j_{\ell_{3}}(k_{3}x_{2})j_{\ell_{1}}(k_{1}x_{3})j_{\ell_{2}}(k_{2}x_{4})

\displaystyle\times\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{4}\,q_{4}[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}})]Y_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{4}*}(-\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})Y_{\lambda_{4}}^{\mu_{4}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{4},|\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}|)A_{-}(\tau_{4},q_{4})

\displaystyle\times\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{1}\,q_{1}[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}})]Y_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{1}*}(-\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})Y_{\lambda_{1}}^{\mu_{1}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{1},|\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}|)A_{-}(\tau_{1},q_{1})

\displaystyle\hskip 290.21811pt\times j_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}(q_{1}x_{3})j_{\lambda_{1}}(q_{1}x_{2}).

Here we have used the notation
\bm{\ell}\equiv(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3},\ell_{4})
and

. This notation also shortens the indexing of the summations, so that we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{m}}\equiv\sum_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\ell_{4}}\sum_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}m_{4}}.

(45)

) and (

). We find

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{J(\tau, \vb k_1) J(\tau, \vb k_2) J(\tau, \vb k_3) J(\tau, \vb k_4)}^{(1)}=

(46)

\displaystyle\frac{4{,}096}{\Lambda^{4}a^{4}(\tau)}\sum_{\mathbf{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{m}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda},\bm{\mu}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda}^{\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}\Bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{4}i^{\ell_{j}+\lambda_{j}+\lambda^{\prime}_{j}}(-1)^{\lambda^{\prime}_{j}+\mu_{j}+\mu^{\prime}_{j}}Y_{\ell_{j}}^{m_{j}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{j})\sum_{\Lambda_{j}M_{j}}(-1)^{M_{j}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{j}\lambda_{j}\Lambda_{j}}^{-\mu^{\prime}_{j}-\mu_{j}-M_{j}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{2}\ell_{4}\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}^{\mu_{2}m_{4}\mu^{\prime}_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{1}\ell_{3}\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}^{\mu_{1}m_{3}\mu^{\prime}_{3}}\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{1}\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{4}}^{m_{1}\mu^{\prime}_{1}\mu_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{2}\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{3}}^{m_{2}\mu^{\prime}_{2}\mu_{3}}

\displaystyle\times\int\int\int\int\differential x_{1}\differential x_{2}\,\differential x_{3}\,\differential x_{4}\,\,x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}^{2}x_{4}^{2}\,\,j_{\ell_{4}}(k_{4}x_{1})j_{\ell_{3}}(k_{3}x_{2})j_{\ell_{1}}(k_{1}x_{3})j_{\ell_{2}}(k_{2}x_{4})

\displaystyle\times\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{4}\,q_{4}[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}})]Y_{\Lambda_{4}}^{M_{4}}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{4},|\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}|)A_{-}(\tau_{4},q_{4})j_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}(q_{4}x_{1})j_{\lambda_{4}}(q_{4}x_{3})

\displaystyle\times\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{3}\,q_{3}[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{3})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}})]Y_{\Lambda_{3}}^{M_{3}}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{3})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{3},|\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}|)A_{-}(\tau_{3},q_{3})j_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}(q_{3}x_{2})j_{\lambda_{3}}(q_{3}x_{4})

\displaystyle\times\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{2}\,q_{2}[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{2})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2}})]Y_{\Lambda_{2}}^{M_{2}}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{2})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{2},|\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2}|)A_{-}(\tau_{2},q_{2})j_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}}(q_{2}x_{4})j_{\lambda_{2}}(q_{2}x_{1})

\displaystyle\times\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{1}\,q_{1}[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}})]Y_{\Lambda_{1}}^{M_{1}}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{1},|\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}|)A_{-}(\tau_{1},q_{1})j_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}(q_{1}x_{3})j_{\lambda_{1}}(q_{1}x_{2}).

Our expression may now be written in the simplified form

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{J(\tau, \vb k_1) J(\tau, \vb k_2) J(\tau, \vb k_3) J(\tau, \vb k_4)}^{(1)}=

(47)

\displaystyle\frac{4{,}096}{\Lambda^{4}a^{4}(\tau)}\sum_{\mathbf{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{m}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda},\bm{\mu}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda}^{\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}\Bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{4}i^{\ell_{j}+\lambda_{j}+\lambda^{\prime}_{j}}(-1)^{\lambda^{\prime}_{j}+\mu_{j}+\mu^{\prime}_{j}}Y_{\ell_{j}}^{m_{j}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{j})\sum_{\Lambda_{j}M_{j}}(-1)^{M_{j}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{j}\lambda_{j}\Lambda_{j}}^{-\mu^{\prime}_{j}-\mu_{j}-M_{j}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{2}\ell_{4}\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}^{\mu_{2}m_{4}\mu^{\prime}_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{1}\ell_{3}\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}^{\mu_{1}m_{3}\mu^{\prime}_{3}}\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{1}\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{4}}^{m_{1}\mu^{\prime}_{1}\mu_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{2}\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{3}}^{m_{2}\mu^{\prime}_{2}\mu_{3}}

\displaystyle\times\int\int\int\int\differential x_{1}\,\differential x_{2}\,\differential x_{3}\,\differential x_{4}\,\,x_{1}^{2}\,x_{2}^{2}\,x_{3}^{2}\,x_{4}^{2}\,\,j_{\ell_{4}}(k_{4}x_{1})j_{\ell_{3}}(k_{3}x_{2})j_{\ell_{1}}(k_{1}x_{3})j_{\ell_{2}}(k_{2}x_{4})

\displaystyle\times\text{I}_{\Lambda_{1}\lambda_{1}\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}^{M_{1}}(\mathbf{k}_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\tau_{1})\text{I}_{\Lambda_{2}\lambda_{2}\lambda^{\prime}_{2}}^{M_{2}}(\mathbf{k}_{2},x_{1},x_{4},\tau_{2})\text{I}_{\Lambda_{3}\lambda_{3}\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}^{M_{3}}(\mathbf{k}_{3},x_{4},x_{2},\tau_{3})\text{I}_{\Lambda_{4}\lambda_{4}\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}^{M_{4}}(\mathbf{k}_{4},x_{3},x_{1},\tau_{4})

where

\displaystyle\text{I}_{\Lambda\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}^{M}(\mathbf{k},x,x^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})\equiv\int\differential[]

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}\ q[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}})]Y_{\Lambda}^{M}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime},|\mathbf{k-q}|)A_{-}(\tau^{\prime},q)j_{\lambda}(qx)j_{\lambda^{\prime}}(qx^{\prime}).

(48)

Inserting this expression into Eq. (
32
), we find that the contribution to the trispectrum due to
Reference Diagram 1
is

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_1) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_2) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_3) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_4)}^{(1)}=

(49)

\displaystyle\frac{4{,}096}{\Lambda^{4}a^{4}(\tau)}\sum_{\mathbf{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{m}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda},\bm{\mu}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda}^{\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}\Bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{4}i^{\ell_{j}+\lambda_{j}+\lambda^{\prime}_{j}}(-1)^{\lambda^{\prime}_{j}+\mu_{j}+\mu^{\prime}_{j}}Y_{\ell_{j}}^{m_{j}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{j})\sum_{\Lambda_{j}M_{j}}(-1)^{M_{j}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{j}\lambda_{j}\Lambda_{j}}^{-\mu^{\prime}_{j}-\mu_{j}-M_{j}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{2}\ell_{4}\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}^{\mu_{2}m_{4}\mu^{\prime}_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{1}\ell_{3}\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}^{\mu_{1}m_{3}\mu^{\prime}_{3}}\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{1}\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{4}}^{m_{1}\mu^{\prime}_{1}\mu_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{2}\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{3}}^{m_{2}\mu^{\prime}_{2}\mu_{3}}

\displaystyle\times\int\int\int\int\differential x_{1}\,\differential x_{2}\,\differential x_{3}\,\differential x_{4}\,\,x_{1}^{2}\,x_{2}^{2}\,x_{3}^{2}\,x_{4}^{2}\,\,j_{\ell_{4}}(k_{4}x_{1})j_{\ell_{3}}(k_{3}x_{2})j_{\ell_{1}}(k_{1}x_{3})j_{\ell_{2}}(k_{2}x_{4})

\displaystyle\times\int_{-\infty}^{0}\differential\tau_{4}\;G(\tau,\tau_{4},k_{4})\text{I}_{\Lambda_{4}\lambda_{4}\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}^{M_{4}}(\mathbf{k}_{4},x_{3},x_{1},\tau_{4})\int_{-\infty}^{0}\differential\tau_{3}\;G(\tau,\tau_{3},k_{3})\text{I}_{\Lambda_{3}\lambda_{3}\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}^{M_{3}}(\mathbf{k}_{3},x_{4},x_{2},\tau_{3})

\displaystyle\times\int_{-\infty}^{0}\differential\tau_{2}\;G(\tau,\tau_{2},k_{2})\text{I}_{\Lambda_{2}\lambda_{2}\lambda^{\prime}_{2}}^{M_{2}}(\mathbf{k}_{2},x_{1},x_{4},\tau_{2})\int_{-\infty}^{0}\differential\tau_{1}\;G(\tau,\tau_{1},k_{1})\text{I}_{\Lambda_{1}\lambda_{1}\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}^{M_{1}}(\mathbf{k}_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\tau_{1}).

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}
{}{}{

\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{-}^{{\dagger}}(-\mathbf{q}_{4})\vbox to22.00002pt{}{}\rangle.

The corresponding source-term correlation function is

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{J(\tau, \vb k_1) J(\tau, \vb k_2) J(\tau, \vb k_3) J(\tau, \vb k_4)}^{(2)}=

(50)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Lambda^{4}a^{4}(\tau)}\int\int\int\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{1}\ \differential[]\mathbf{q}_{2}\ \differential[]\mathbf{q}_{3}\ \differential[]\mathbf{q}_{4}\;q_{1}\;q_{2}\;q_{3}\;q_{4}[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}})][\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{2})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2}})]

\displaystyle\times[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{3})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}})][\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}})]\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4})

\displaystyle\times\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k_{3}-q_{3}+q_{2}})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{3}+\mathbf{q}_{4})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{1},|\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}|)A_{-}(\tau_{1},q_{1})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{2},|\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2}|)A_{-}(\tau_{2},q_{2})

\displaystyle\times A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{3},|\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}|)A_{-}(\tau_{3},q_{3})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{4},|\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}|)A_{-}(\tau_{4},q_{4}).

We proceed in precisely the same manner as in the previous case, expanding the Dirac delta functions. This time we must also employ Eqs. (
141
) and (
147
). We have

\displaystyle\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k_{3}-q_{3}+q_{2}})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{3}+\mathbf{q}_{4})

(51)

\displaystyle=32\pi\sum_{\mathbf{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{m}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda},\bm{\mu}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda}^{\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}\sum_{A,B}i^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\lambda^{\prime}_{1}+\lambda^{\prime}_{2}}\mathcal{K}_{\ell_{1}\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\ell_{2}\lambda^{\prime}_{2}A}^{m_{1}\mu^{\prime}_{1}m_{2}\mu^{\prime}_{2}B}\mathcal{R}_{\ell_{1}\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\ell_{2}\lambda^{\prime}_{2}A}(k_{1},q_{1},k_{2},q_{2})

\displaystyle\times Y_{\ell_{1}}^{m_{1}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{1})Y_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{1}*}(-\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})Y_{\ell_{2}}^{m_{2}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{2})Y_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{2}*}(-\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{2})

\displaystyle\times 8i^{\lambda_{1}+\ell_{4}+\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{1}\ell_{4}\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}^{\mu_{1}m_{4}\mu^{\prime}_{4}}\mathcal{R}_{\lambda_{1}\ell_{4}\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}(q_{1},k_{4},q_{4})Y_{\lambda_{1}}^{\mu_{1}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})Y_{\ell_{4}}^{m_{4}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{4})Y_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{4}*}(-\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})

\displaystyle\times 8i^{\ell_{3}+\lambda^{\prime}_{3}+\lambda_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{3}\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{2}}^{m_{3}\mu^{\prime}_{3}\mu_{2}}\mathcal{R}_{\ell_{3}\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{2}}(k_{3},q_{3},q_{2})Y_{\ell_{3}}^{m_{3}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{3})Y_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{3}*}(-\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{3})Y_{\lambda_{2}}^{\mu_{2}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{2})

\displaystyle\times\frac{2}{\pi}(-1)^{\lambda_{3}}\mathcal{R}_{\lambda_{3}\lambda_{4}}(q_{3},q_{4})Y_{\lambda_{3}}^{\mu_{3}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{3})Y_{\lambda_{4}}^{\mu_{4}}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})\delta^{\rm K}_{\lambda_{3}\lambda_{4}}\delta^{\rm K}_{\mu_{3}\mu_{4}},

where
\mathcal{K}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\ell_{4}L}^{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}m_{4}M}
is defined in Appendix
A
.
After using Eq. (
51
) in Eq. (
50
), we obtain

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{J(\tau, \vb k_1) J(\tau, \vb k_2) J(\tau, \vb k_3) J(\tau, \vb k_4)}^{(2)}=

(52)

\displaystyle\frac{4{,}096}{\Lambda^{4}a^{4}(\tau)}\sum_{\mathbf{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{m}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda},\bm{\mu}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda}^{\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}\sum_{A,B}\Bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{4}i^{\ell_{j}+\lambda_{j}+\lambda^{\prime}_{j}}Y_{\ell_{j}}^{m_{j}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{j})\Bigg]\mathcal{K}_{\ell_{1}\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\ell_{2}\lambda^{\prime}_{2}A}^{m_{1}\mu^{\prime}_{1}m_{2}\mu^{\prime}_{2}B}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{1}\ell_{4}\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}^{\mu_{1}m_{4}\mu^{\prime}_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{3}\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{2}}^{m_{3}\mu^{\prime}_{3}\mu_{2}}\delta^{\rm K}_{\lambda_{3}\lambda_{4}}\delta^{\rm K}_{\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}

\displaystyle\times\int\int\int\int\differential x_{1}\,\differential x_{2}\,\differential x_{3}\,\differential x_{4}\,\,x_{1}^{2}\,x_{2}^{2}\,x_{3}^{2}\,x_{4}^{2}\,\,j_{\ell_{1}}(k_{1}x_{1})j_{\ell_{2}}(k_{2}x_{1})j_{\ell_{4}}(k_{4}x_{2})j_{\ell_{3}}(k_{3}x_{3})

\displaystyle\times\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{4}\,q_{4}[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}})]Y_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{4}*}(-\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})Y_{\lambda_{4}}^{\mu_{4}}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{4},|\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}|)A_{-}(\tau_{4},q_{4})

\displaystyle\hskip 290.21811pt\times j_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}(q_{4}x_{2})j_{\lambda_{4}}(q_{4}x_{4})

\displaystyle\times\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{3}\,q_{3}[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{3})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}})]Y_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{3}*}(-\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{3})Y_{\lambda_{3}}^{\mu_{3}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{3})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{3},|\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}|)A_{-}(\tau_{3},q_{3})

\displaystyle\hskip 290.21811pt\times j_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}(q_{3}x_{3})j_{\lambda_{3}}(q_{3}x_{4})

\displaystyle\times\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{2}\,q_{2}[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{2})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2}})]Y_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{2}*}(-\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{2})Y_{\lambda_{2}}^{\mu_{2}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{2})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{2},|\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2}|)A_{-}(\tau_{2},q_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 290.21811pt\times j_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}}(q_{2}x_{1})j_{\lambda_{2}}(q_{2}x_{3})

\displaystyle\times\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{1}\,q_{1}[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}})]Y_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{1}*}(-\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})Y_{\lambda_{1}}^{\mu_{1}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{1},|\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}|)A_{-}(\tau_{1},q_{1})

\displaystyle\hskip 290.21811pt\times j_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}(q_{1}x_{1})j_{\lambda_{1}}(q_{1}x_{2}).

We again linearize the spherical harmonics, finding

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{J(\tau, \vb k_1) J(\tau, \vb k_2) J(\tau, \vb k_3) J(\tau, \vb k_4)}^{(2)}=

(53)

\displaystyle\frac{4{,}096}{\Lambda^{4}a^{4}(\tau)}\sum_{\mathbf{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{m}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda},\bm{\mu}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda}^{\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}\sum_{A,B}\Bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{4}i^{\ell_{j}+\lambda_{j}+\lambda^{\prime}_{j}}Y_{\ell_{j}}^{m_{j}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{j})\sum_{\Lambda_{j}}\sum_{M_{j}}(-1)^{M_{j}}\Bigg]\mathcal{K}_{\ell_{1}\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\ell_{2}\lambda^{\prime}_{2}A}^{m_{1}\mu^{\prime}_{1}m_{2}\mu^{\prime}_{2}B}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{1}\ell_{4}\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}^{\mu_{1}m_{4}\mu^{\prime}_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{3}\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{2}}^{m_{3}\mu^{\prime}_{3}\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle\times\delta^{\rm K}_{\lambda_{3}\lambda_{4}}\delta^{\rm K}_{\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}(-1)^{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}+\mu^{\prime}_{4}}(-1)^{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}+\mu_{3}+\mu^{\prime}_{3}}(-1)^{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}+\mu_{2}+\mu^{\prime}_{2}}(-1)^{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}+\mu_{1}+\mu^{\prime}_{1}}

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{G}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}\lambda_{4}\Lambda_{4}}^{-\mu^{\prime}_{4}\mu_{4}-M_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{3}\Lambda_{3}}^{-\mu^{\prime}_{3}-\mu_{3}-M_{3}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{2}\Lambda_{2}}^{-\mu^{\prime}_{2}-\mu_{2}-M_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{1}\Lambda_{1}}^{-\mu^{\prime}_{1}-\mu_{1}-M_{1}}

\displaystyle\times\int\int\int\int\differential x_{1}\,\differential x_{2}\,\differential x_{3}\,\differential x_{4}\,\,x_{1}^{2}\,x_{2}^{2}\,x_{3}^{2}\,x_{4}^{2}\,\,j_{\ell_{1}}(k_{1}x_{1})j_{\ell_{2}}(k_{2}x_{1})j_{\ell_{4}}(k_{4}x_{2})j_{\ell_{3}}(k_{3}x_{3})

\displaystyle\times\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{4}\,q_{4}[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}})]Y_{\Lambda_{4}}^{M_{4}}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{4},|\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}|)A_{-}(\tau_{4},q_{4})j_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}(q_{4}x_{2})j_{\lambda_{4}}(q_{4}x_{4})

\displaystyle\times\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{3}\,q_{3}[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{3})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}})]Y_{\Lambda_{3}}^{M_{3}}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{3})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{3},|\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}|)A_{-}(\tau_{3},q_{3})j_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}(q_{3}x_{3})j_{\lambda_{3}}(q_{3}x_{4})

\displaystyle\times\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{2}\,q_{2}[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{2})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2}})]Y_{\Lambda_{2}}^{M_{2}}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{2})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{2},|\mathbf{k}_{2}-\mathbf{q}_{2}|)A_{-}(\tau_{2},q_{2})j_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}}(q_{2}x_{1})j_{\lambda_{2}}(q_{2}x_{3})

\displaystyle\times\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{1}\,q_{1}[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}})]Y_{\Lambda_{1}}^{M_{1}}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{1})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau_{1},|\mathbf{k}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{1}|)A_{-}(\tau_{1},q_{1})j_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}(q_{1}x_{1})j_{\lambda_{1}}(q_{1}x_{2}).

Thus the contribution to the trispectrum due to
Reference Diagram 2
is

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_1) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_2) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_3) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_4)}^{(2)}=

(54)

\displaystyle\frac{4{,}096}{\Lambda^{4}a^{4}(\tau)}\sum_{\mathbf{\bm{\ell}},\mathbf{m}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda},\bm{\mu}}\sum_{\bm{\lambda}^{\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}}\sum_{A,B}\Bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{4}i^{\ell_{j}+\lambda_{j}+\lambda^{\prime}_{j}}(-1)^{\lambda^{\prime}_{j}+\mu_{j}+\mu^{\prime}_{j}}Y_{\ell_{j}}^{m_{j}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{j})\sum_{\Lambda_{j}}\sum_{M_{j}}(-1)^{M_{j}}\Bigg](-1)^{\mu_{4}}

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{K}_{\ell_{1}\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\ell_{2}\lambda^{\prime}_{2}A}^{m_{1}\mu^{\prime}_{1}m_{2}\mu^{\prime}_{2}B}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda_{1}\ell_{4}\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}^{\mu_{1}m_{4}\mu^{\prime}_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{3}\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{2}}^{m_{3}\mu^{\prime}_{3}\mu_{2}}\delta^{\rm K}_{\lambda_{3}\lambda_{4}}\delta^{\rm K}_{\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}\lambda_{4}\Lambda_{4}}^{-\mu^{\prime}_{4}\mu_{4}-M_{4}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{3}\Lambda_{3}}^{-\mu^{\prime}_{3}-\mu_{3}-M_{3}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{2}\Lambda_{2}}^{-\mu^{\prime}_{2}-\mu_{2}-M_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{1}\Lambda_{1}}^{-\mu^{\prime}_{1}-\mu_{1}-M_{1}}

\displaystyle\times\int\int\int\int\differential x_{1}\,\differential x_{2}\,\differential x_{3}\,\differential x_{4}\,\,x_{1}^{2}\,x_{2}^{2}\,x_{3}^{2}\,x_{4}^{2}\,\,j_{\ell_{1}}(k_{1}x_{1})j_{\ell_{2}}(k_{2}x_{1})j_{\ell_{4}}(k_{4}x_{2})j_{\ell_{3}}(k_{3}x_{3})

\displaystyle\times\int_{-\infty}^{0}\differential\tau_{4}\;G(\tau,\tau_{4},k_{4})\text{I}_{\Lambda_{4}\lambda_{4}\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}^{M_{4}}(\mathbf{k}_{4},x_{4},x_{2},\tau_{4})\int_{-\infty}^{0}\differential\tau_{3}\;G(\tau,\tau_{3},k_{3})\text{I}_{\Lambda_{3}\lambda_{3}\lambda^{\prime}_{3}}^{M_{3}}(\mathbf{k}_{3},x_{4},x_{3},\tau_{3})

\displaystyle\times\int_{-\infty}^{0}\differential\tau_{2}\;G(\tau,\tau_{2},k_{2})\text{I}_{\Lambda_{2}\lambda_{2}\lambda^{\prime}_{2}}^{M_{2}}(\mathbf{k}_{2},x_{3},x_{1},\tau_{2})\int_{-\infty}^{0}\differential\tau_{1}\;G(\tau,\tau_{1},k_{1})\text{I}_{\Lambda_{1}\lambda_{1}\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}^{M_{1}}(\mathbf{k}_{1},x_{2},x_{1},\tau_{1}).
