9
Numerical Integration Scheme for the Radial Integrals

The integrals we encountered in §
7
and §
8
cannot be computed analytically; we will need to turn to numerical methods. There is extensive literature on numerical integration of spherical Bessel functions
[
62
,
63
]
; we refer the reader to
[
64
]
for a recent fast method that exploits rotation in the space of free frequencies to reduce dimensionality, as well as
[
65
,
66
,
67
,
68
,
69
,
70
]
using the FFTLog algorithm.

We defer a full numerical implementation of the expressions of this work to the second paper in this series. Here we restrict ourselves to a brief discussion to make it clear that the expressions we have obtained carry with them minimal computational cost, especially in comparison to the naive 16-dimensional integrations required when the loop integrals are first written down.

The integrals
\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda_{i}\lambda_{i}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}}^{M}(\mathbf{k}_{i},x_{j},x_{k},\tau)
given in Eq. (
115
) can be computed using the 2D FFTLog algorithm
[
71
]
, after which
\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda_{i}\lambda_{i}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}}^{M}(\mathbf{k}_{i},x_{j},x_{k},\tau)
will be represented by an
n\times m\times m
tensor (
\tau
assumes a single value, corresponding to the end of inflation). One must then integrate these tensors against spherical Bessel functions as shown in §
8
. In principle these latter integrals could also be done in this way except that code to do FFTLog in more than two arguments does not yet exist. So instead, we return to the simple method of Riemann sums for the subsequent integrations. This enables numerical computation in
python
using the
numpy
library’s
einsum
function. A method using these functions was first presented in
[
27
]
and we briefly review it here.

We may represent each
k_{i}\in\{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}\}
as an
n
-dimensional numerical vector, and
x_{i}\in\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\}
as an
m
-dimensional numerical vector. Then each spherical Bessel function
j_{\ell_{i}}(k_{i}x_{j})
will be an
n\times m
matrix for a fixed value of
\ell_{i}
. In §
8
, these functions must then be integrated over. Let us consider as an example the integral over
x_{4}
in Eq. (
117
):

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential x_{4}\,x_{4}^{2}\,j_{\ell_{2}}(k_{2}x_{4})\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda_{2}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}^{M_{2}}(\mathbf{k}_{2},x_{1},x_{4},\tau)\mathcal{I}_{\Lambda_{3}\lambda_{3}\lambda_{3}^{\prime}}^{M_{3}}(\mathbf{k}_{3},x_{4},x_{2},\tau).

The integrand contains the
m
-dimensional numerical vector
x_{4}
, an
n\times m
matrix
j_{\ell_{2}}(k_{2}x_{4})
and two
n\times m\times m
tensors. We may numerically integrate these functions simply by contracting the arrays along the dimension corresponding to
x_{4}
(and multiplying the result by the discretized differential
dx_{4}
). This operation may be done conveniently with the
einsum
function of
python
’s
numpy
library. The result is a 4D array, with the indices corresponding to the values of
k_{2}
,
k_{3}
,
x_{1}
, and
x_{2}
. This same method can be used to compute all of the integrals of §
8
.
The most complicated integrals that we must compute numerically are 6D, such as the integral over
x_{2}
in Eq. (
117
).

10
Discussion & Conclusions

Recent measurements of the galaxy 4PCF have found evidence for parity violation in the 3D large-scale structure
[
20
,
21
]
. If true, this finding indicates that there was very likely a parity-violating interaction during inflation. An inflationary model with an axion coupled to a
U(1)
gauge field is a leading candidate for such a parity-violating theory. The primordial trispectrum only has a parity-odd component at one-loop and beyond; the loop integrals that appear in this calculation are extremely difficult to calculate analytically, and are also very expensive if one sought to do them fully numerically. We have demonstrated a new method to simplify this computation, which relies on rewriting the Dirac delta functions using the plane wave expansion and making use of the Convolution Theorem. We have drastically reduced the dimensionality of the trispectrum integrals.

One previous work computed the trispectrum for the model of axion inflation we explore here; however they were restricted to just two wave-vector configurations due to the high cost of numerically evaluating the loop integrals
[
39
]
. Another work that computed the trispectrum for a similar model, but with an axion as a spectator field, also explored only a small portion of the parameter space
[
40
]
.

With the method presented in this paper, it is now feasible to compute these integrals numerically and thus calculate the primordial trispectrum of axion inflation for all configurations. One could use this method to compare with recent lattice simulations of the axion inflation trispectrum
[
38
,
72
,
73
]
. Our analytic treatment assumes
\xi
is constant in time; a lattice simulation would not need to make this assumption.

The ultimate aim of our work is constraining axion inflation through fitting a model of the galaxy 4PCF from it to the measured 4PCF. In future work, we will develop code that implements the methods outlined in this paper, and numerically compute the primordial trispectrum for a large range of the parameter space. Next we will apply the linear transfer function to evolve the primordial trispectrum in time. After accounting for galaxy biasing, applying redshift space distortions, and taking an inverse FT, we will obtain the galaxy 4PCF. With the galaxy 4PCF in hand, one can determine the tetrahedra that have the highest parity-odd signal to noise ratio. Considering only these tetrahedra will greatly reduce the number of degrees of freedom in our covariance matrix, significantly reducing the uncertainty as compared to previous galaxy 4PCF measurements (
[
74
]
, page 4). This will allow us to place the first ever constraints on parity violation from axion inflation using higher-order galaxy correlations.

Appendix A
Expansions of the Multi-Argument Dirac Delta Function

We will rewrite the Dirac delta functions of multiple arguments that occur in the main text in a form more suitable for our trispectrum calculations.

A.1
Definitions

Our expansions decompose the Dirac delta functions into a sum of angular coefficients multiplied by radial integrals; we define these expressions now. The Gaunt integral is

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}^{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}}\equiv\int Y_{\ell_{1}}^{m_{1}}(\hat{\mathbf{r}})Y_{\ell_{2}}^{m_{2}}(\hat{\mathbf{r}})Y_{\ell_{3}}^{m_{3}}(\hat{\mathbf{r}})\differential\Omega_{r}

(129)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\sqrt{\frac{(2\ell_{1}+1)(2\ell_{2}+1)(2\ell_{3}+1)}{4\pi}}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix \ell_1 & \ell_2 & \ell_3 \\ 0 & 0 & 0\endlx@physics@matrix)\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix \ell_1 & \ell_2 & \ell_3 \\ m_1 & m_2 & m_3\endlx@physics@matrix),

where the
2\times 3
matrices are Wigner 3-
j
symbols
6
6

6

https://dlmf.nist.gov/34.3

[
75
]
, representing the addition of angular momenta. Each
z
-component is bounded by its corresponding total angular momentum. Non-zero entries in the bottom row of a 3-
j
symbol describe the
z
-component of the angular momentum. The Gaunt integral vanishes unless the angular momenta satisfy the triangle inequality
|\ell_{1}-\ell_{2}|\leq\ell_{3}\leq\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}
and the
z
-component of the sum of the angular momenta vanishes,
m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}=0
. A 3-
j
with an identically zero bottom row requires that
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}
be even.

In the expansion of the four-argument Dirac delta function, we will have the coefficient

\mathcal{K}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\ell_{4}L}^{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}m_{4}M}\equiv(-1)^{M}(2L+1)\sqrt{\frac{(2\ell_{1}+1)(2\ell_{2}+1)(2\ell_{3}+1)(2\ell_{4}+1)}{4\pi}}\\
\times\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix \ell_3 & \ell_4 & L \\ m_3 & m_4 & M\endlx@physics@matrix)\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix \ell_3 & \ell_4 & L \\ 0 & 0 & 0\endlx@physics@matrix)\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix \ell_1 & \ell_2 & L \\ 0 & 0 & 0\endlx@physics@matrix)\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix \ell_1 & \ell_2 & L \\ m_1 & m_2 & -M\endlx@physics@matrix).

(130)

Additionally, we will have the three radial integrals:

\mathcal{R}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}(k,p)\equiv\int\differential x\ x^{2}j_{\ell_{1}}(kx)j_{\ell_{2}}(px),

(131)

\mathcal{R}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}(k,p,q)\equiv\int\differential x\ x^{2}j_{\ell_{1}}(kx)j_{\ell_{2}}(px)j_{\ell_{3}}(qx),

(132)

and

\mathcal{R}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\ell_{4}}(k,p,q,s)\equiv\int\differential x\ x^{2}j_{\ell_{1}}(kx)j_{\ell_{2}}(px)j_{\ell_{3}}(qx)j_{\ell_{4}}(sx).

(133)

We note that Eq. (
132
) can be obtained by taking limit as
s\to 0
of Eq. (
133
), and (
131
) can be obtained by taking the limit as
q\to 0
of (
132
).

A.2
Two-Argument Dirac Delta Expansion

The Dirac delta function in Fourier space can be written as an FT of unity:

(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{p})=\int\differential[]\mathbf{x}\ e^{i\mathbf{q}\dotproduct\mathbf{x}}e^{i\mathbf{p}\dotproduct\mathbf{x}}.

(134)

We proceed by using the plane wave expansion:

e^{i\mathbf{q}\dotproduct\mathbf{x}}=4\pi\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}i^{\ell}j_{\ell}(qx)Y_{\ell}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})Y_{\ell}^{m*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}).

(135)

We note that we can exchange the complex conjugate between either spherical harmonic in (
135
) due to commutativity of the dot product.
Spherical harmonics have several properties we will use, which we list here:

Y_{\ell}^{m*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})=(-1)^{m}Y_{\ell}^{-m}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}),\hskip 14.22636ptY_{\ell}^{m}(-\hat{\mathbf{q}})=(-1)^{\ell}Y_{l}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}),

(136)

Y_{\ell}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})Y_{\ell^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})=\sum_{L=0}^{\infty}\sum_{M=-L}^{L}(-1)^{M}\mathcal{G}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}L}^{mm^{\prime}-M}Y_{L}^{M}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}),

(137)

where
\mathcal{G}
is defined in Eq. (
129
). We will call the two properties in Eq. (
136
) conjugation and parity, respectively, and the property (
137
) linearization. From Eq. (
137
) one may also derive the analogous expression for linearization of products of associated Legendre polynomials,
P_{L}^{M}
:

\displaystyle P_{\ell}^{m}(\cos\theta)P_{\ell^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}(\cos\theta)

(138)

\displaystyle=\sum_{L=0}^{\infty}\sum_{M=-L}^{L}(-1)^{M}\sqrt{\frac{4\pi(2L+1)}{(2\ell+1)(2\ell^{\prime}+1)}\frac{(\ell+m)!(\ell^{\prime}+m^{\prime})!(L-M)!}{(\ell-m)!(\ell^{\prime}-m^{\prime})!(L+M)!}}\;\mathcal{G}_{\ell\ell^{\prime}L}^{mm^{\prime}-M}P_{L}^{M}(\cos\theta).

Applying the expansion (
135
) to Eq. (
134
) yields

(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{p})=(4\pi)^{2}\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\sum_{m_{1},m_{2}}i^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}}Y_{\ell_{1}}^{m_{1}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})Y_{\ell_{2}}^{m_{2}}(\hat{\mathbf{p}})\int\differential\Omega_{x}Y_{\ell_{1}}^{m_{1}}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})Y_{\ell_{2}}^{m_{2}*}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})\\
\times\int\differential x\ x^{2}j_{\ell_{1}}(qx)j_{\ell_{2}}(px).

(139)

Applying the orthogonality of the spherical harmonics,

\int\differential\Omega_{x}Y_{\ell_{1}}^{m_{1}}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})Y_{\ell_{2}}^{m_{2}*}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})=\delta^{\rm K}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}\delta^{\rm K}_{m_{1}m_{2}},

(140)

we have

\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{p})=\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{\ell}\mathcal{R}_{\ell\ell}(q,p)\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}Y_{\ell}^{m*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})Y_{\ell}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{p}}).

(141)

A.3
Three-Argument Dirac Delta Expansion

We begin by writing a Dirac delta function in Fourier space with three arguments as a forward FT:

(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{p})=\int\differential[]\mathbf{x}\ e^{i\mathbf{k}\dotproduct\mathbf{x}}e^{i\mathbf{q}\dotproduct\mathbf{x}}e^{i\mathbf{p}\dotproduct\mathbf{x}};

(142)

we then proceed as before. Making use of the plane wave expansion (
135
) we have

(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{p})=(4\pi)^{3}\sum_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}\sum_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}}i^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}}Y_{\ell_{1}}^{m_{1}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}})Y_{\ell_{2}}^{m_{2}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})Y_{\ell_{3}}^{m_{3}*}(\hat{\mathbf{p}})\\
\times\int\differential\Omega_{x}Y_{\ell_{1}}^{m_{1}}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})Y_{\ell_{2}}^{m_{2}}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})Y_{\ell_{3}}^{m_{3}}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})\int\differential x\ x^{2}j_{\ell_{1}}(kx)j_{\ell_{2}}(qx)j_{\ell_{3}}(px).

(143)

We may rewrite this using Eqs. (
129
) and (
132
) to obtain

\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{p})=8\sum_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}\sum_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}}i^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}}\mathcal{G}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}^{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}}\mathcal{R}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}}(k,q,p)Y_{\ell_{1}}^{m_{1}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}})Y_{\ell_{2}}^{m_{2}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})Y_{\ell_{3}}^{m_{3}*}(\hat{\mathbf{p}}).

(144)

A.4
Four-Argument Dirac Delta Expansion

We proceed as in the previous two subsections. Writing the four-argument Dirac delta function in Fourier space as a forward FT, we have

(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{s})=\int\differential[]\mathbf{x}\ e^{i\mathbf{k}\dotproduct\mathbf{x}}e^{i\mathbf{q}\dotproduct\mathbf{x}}e^{i\mathbf{p}\dotproduct\mathbf{x}}e^{i\mathbf{s}\dotproduct\mathbf{x}}

(145)

=(4\pi)^{4}\sum_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\ell_{4}}\;\sum_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}m_{4}}i^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}+\ell_{4}}Y_{\ell_{1}}^{m_{1}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}})Y_{\ell_{2}}^{m_{2}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})Y_{\ell_{3}}^{m_{3}*}(\hat{\mathbf{p}})Y_{\ell_{4}}^{m_{4}*}(\hat{\mathbf{s}})\\
\times\int\differential\Omega_{x}\;Y_{\ell_{1}}^{m_{1}}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})Y_{\ell_{2}}^{m_{2}}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})Y_{\ell_{3}}^{m_{3}}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})Y_{\ell_{4}}^{m_{4}}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})\int\differential x\ x^{2}j_{\ell_{1}}(kx)j_{\ell_{2}}(qx)j_{\ell_{3}}(px)j_{\ell_{4}}(sx)

(146)

=(4\pi)^{4}\sum_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\ell_{4}}\;\sum_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}m_{4}}\;\sum_{L,M}i^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}+\ell_{4}}\mathcal{K}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\ell_{4}L}^{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}m_{4}M}\mathcal{R}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\ell_{4}}(k,q,p,s)\\
\times Y_{\ell_{1}}^{m_{1}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}})Y_{\ell_{2}}^{m_{2}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})Y_{\ell_{3}}^{m_{3}*}(\hat{\mathbf{p}})Y_{\ell_{4}}^{m_{4}*}(\hat{\mathbf{s}}),

(147)

where we used the linearization rule (
137
) to combine two of the spherical harmonics into a sum of single spherical harmonics. We find

\displaystyle\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{s})=32\pi\sum_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\ell_{4}}\;\sum_{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}m_{4}}\;\sum_{LM}i^{\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}+\ell_{4}}\mathcal{K}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\ell_{4}L}^{m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}m_{4}M}\mathcal{R}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell_{3}\ell_{4}}(k,q,p,s)

\displaystyle\hskip 221.93149pt\times Y_{\ell_{1}}^{m_{1}*}(\hat{\mathbf{k}})Y_{\ell_{2}}^{m_{2}*}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})Y_{\ell_{3}}^{m_{3}*}(\hat{\mathbf{p}})Y_{\ell_{4}}^{m_{4}*}(\hat{\mathbf{s}}).

(148)

These Dirac delta function expansions are equivalent to those given in
[
76
]
; we also note this expansion for three arguments was used in
[
77
]
, with the original idea going back to
[
78
]
.

Appendix B
Convolutions

The convolution of two functions
f(\mathbf{q})
and
g(\mathbf{q})
, evaluated at a 3-vector
\mathbf{k}
, is defined as

\displaystyle[f\star g](\mathbf{k})=\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}\ f(\mathbf{q})g(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}).

(149)

The Convolution Theorem states that

\displaystyle[f\star g](\mathbf{k})=\mathcal{F}\left\{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\{f(\mathbf{q})\}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\{g(\mathbf{q})\}\right\}(\mathbf{k}).

(150)

Here
\mathcal{F}
represents an FT and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}
an inverse FT.
Eq. (
150
) allows us to convert between convolutions and FTs. We make repeated use of this theorem in §
6
. Here we list all of the convolutional integrals that appear in the computation of the inverse decay trispectrum. Specifically, the angular parts of these integrals come from the expansion of the polarization vector dot product in Eq. (
64
). Analytical simplification of these angular integrals is essential in order to numerically compute the trispectrum for a large portion of the parameter space.
Here we rewrite each term as a sequence of FTs using the Convolution Theorem.

\displaystyle\text{I}_{\Lambda\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}^{M(1)}(\mathbf{k},x,x^{\prime},\tau)\equiv\frac{1}{2}\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}\ Y_{\Lambda}^{M}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})\cos\alpha\cos\theta\cos\phi\cos\beta\,qA_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau,|\mathbf{k-q}|)A_{-}(\tau,q)j_{\lambda}(qx)j_{\lambda^{\prime}}(qx^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip 76.82243pt=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{F}\Bigg\{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\bigg\{\cos\theta\cos\phi Y_{\Lambda}^{M}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})qA_{-}(\tau,q)j_{\lambda}(qx)j_{\lambda^{\prime}}(qx^{\prime})\bigg\}(\mathbf{r})

(151)

\displaystyle\hskip 93.89409pt\times\mathcal{F}^{-1}\bigg\{\cos\theta\cos\phi A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau,q)\bigg\}(\mathbf{r})\Bigg\}(\mathbf{k}),

\displaystyle\text{I}_{\Lambda\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}^{M(2)}(\mathbf{k},x,x^{\prime},\tau)\equiv\frac{1}{2}\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}\ Y_{\Lambda}^{M}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})\cos\alpha\cos\theta\sin\phi\sin\beta\,qA_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau,|\mathbf{k-q}|)A_{-}(\tau,q)j_{\lambda}(qx)j_{\lambda^{\prime}}(qx^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip 76.82243pt=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{F}\Bigg\{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\bigg\{\cos\theta\sin\phi\,Y_{\Lambda}^{M}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})qA_{-}(\tau,q)j_{\lambda}(qx)j_{\lambda^{\prime}}(qx^{\prime})\bigg\}(\mathbf{r})

(152)

\displaystyle\hskip 93.89409pt\times\mathcal{F}^{-1}\bigg\{\cos\theta\sin\phi\,A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau,q)\bigg\}(\mathbf{r})\Bigg\}(\mathbf{k}),

\displaystyle\text{I}_{\Lambda\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}^{M(3)}(\mathbf{k},x,x^{\prime},\tau)\equiv-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}\ Y_{\Lambda}^{M}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})\cos\phi\cos\beta\,qA_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau,|\mathbf{k-q}|)A_{-}(\tau,q)j_{\lambda}(qx)j_{\lambda^{\prime}}(qx^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip 76.82243pt=-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{F}\Bigg\{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\bigg\{\cos\phi\,Y_{\Lambda}^{M}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})qA_{-}(\tau,q)j_{\lambda}(qx)j_{\lambda^{\prime}}(qx^{\prime})\bigg\}(\mathbf{r})

(153)

\displaystyle\hskip 93.89409pt\times\mathcal{F}^{-1}\bigg\{\cos\phi\,A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau,q)\bigg\}(\mathbf{r})\Bigg\}(\mathbf{k}),

\displaystyle\text{I}_{\Lambda\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}^{M(4)}(\mathbf{k},x,x^{\prime},\tau)\equiv-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}\ Y_{\Lambda}^{M}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})\sin\phi\sin\beta\,qA_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau,|\mathbf{k-q}|)A_{-}(\tau,q)j_{\lambda}(qx)j_{\lambda^{\prime}}(qx^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip 76.82243pt=-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{F}\Bigg\{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\bigg\{\sin\phi\,Y_{\Lambda}^{M}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})qA_{-}(\tau,q)j_{\lambda}(qx)j_{\lambda^{\prime}}(qx^{\prime})\bigg\}(\mathbf{r})

(154)

\displaystyle\hskip 93.89409pt\times\mathcal{F}^{-1}\bigg\{\sin\phi\,A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau,q)\bigg\}(\mathbf{r})\Bigg\}(\mathbf{k}),

\displaystyle\text{I}_{\Lambda\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}^{M(5)}(\mathbf{k},x,x^{\prime},\tau)\equiv\frac{1}{2}\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}\ Y_{\Lambda}^{M}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})\sin\alpha\sin\theta\,qA_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau,|\mathbf{k-q}|)A_{-}(\tau,q)j_{\lambda}(qx)j_{\lambda^{\prime}}(qx^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip 76.82243pt=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{F}\Bigg\{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\bigg\{\sin\theta\,Y_{\Lambda}^{M}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})qA_{-}(\tau,q)j_{\lambda}(qx)j_{\lambda^{\prime}}(qx^{\prime})\bigg\}(\mathbf{r})

(155)

\displaystyle\hskip 93.89409pt\times\mathcal{F}^{-1}\bigg\{\sin\theta\,A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau,q)\bigg\}(\mathbf{r})\Bigg\}(\mathbf{k}),

\displaystyle\text{I}_{\Lambda\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}^{M(6)}(\mathbf{k},x,x^{\prime},\tau)\equiv-\frac{i}{2}\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}\;\bigg(\hat{\mathbf{q}}\crossproduct\frac{\mathbf{k-q}}{|\mathbf{k-q}|}\dotproduct\hat{\mathbf{z}}\bigg)\frac{q\cos\alpha}{\sin\alpha\sin\theta}Y_{\Lambda}^{M}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau,|\mathbf{k-q}|)A_{-}(\tau,q)

\displaystyle\hskip 335.74251pt\times j_{\lambda}(qx)j_{\lambda^{\prime}}(qx^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip 76.82243pt=\frac{\sqrt{2}(4\pi)^{3/2}}{6}\sum_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}C_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}^{111}Y_{1}^{\nu_{1}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})\sum_{L^{\prime},M^{\prime}}^{\infty}(-1)^{M^{\prime}}\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda 1L^{\prime}}^{M\nu_{2}-M^{\prime}}

(156)

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{F}\Bigg\{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\bigg\{\frac{1}{\sin\theta}Y_{L^{\prime}}^{M^{\prime}}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})qA_{-}(\tau,q)j_{\lambda}(qx)j_{\lambda^{\prime}}(qx^{\prime})\bigg\}(\mathbf{r})\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left\{\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}Y_{1}^{m_{3}}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau,q)\right\}(\mathbf{r})\Bigg\}(\mathbf{k}),

\displaystyle\text{I}_{\Lambda\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}^{M(7)}(\mathbf{k},x,x^{\prime},\tau)\equiv\frac{i}{2}\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}\;\bigg(\hat{\mathbf{q}}\crossproduct\frac{\mathbf{k-q}}{|\mathbf{k-q}|}\dotproduct\hat{\mathbf{z}}\bigg)\frac{q\cos\theta}{\sin\alpha\sin\theta}Y_{\Lambda}^{M}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau,|\mathbf{k-q}|)A_{-}(\tau,q)

\displaystyle\hskip 335.74251pt\times j_{\lambda}(qx)j_{\lambda^{\prime}}(qx^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip 76.82243pt=-\frac{\sqrt{2}(4\pi)^{3/2}}{6}\sum_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}C_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu_{3}}^{111}Y_{1}^{\nu_{1}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})\sum_{L^{\prime},M^{\prime}}^{\infty}(-1)^{M^{\prime}}\mathcal{G}_{\Lambda 1L^{\prime}}^{M\nu_{2}-M^{\prime}}

(157)

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{F}\Bigg\{\mathcal{F}^{-1}\bigg\{\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}Y_{L^{\prime}}^{M^{\prime}}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})qA_{-}(\tau,q)j_{\lambda}(qx)j_{\lambda^{\prime}}(qx^{\prime})\bigg\}(\mathbf{r})\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{\sin\theta}Y_{1}^{m_{3}}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})A_{-}^{{}^{\prime}}(\tau,q)\right\}(\mathbf{r})\Bigg\}(\mathbf{k}).

Appendix C
Angular Integrals

C.1
General Result

In §
6.2
, we introduced the coefficients (
88
), corresponding to the integral of a spherical harmonic against another function:

\displaystyle C_{(i)\alpha}^{(j)\beta}\equiv\int\differential\Omega\ Y_{\alpha}^{\beta}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})\mathbf{f}_{i}^{(j)}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}).

(158)

where
\Omega
is the solid angle explored by the
\hat{\mathbf{q}}
vector, and
\mathbf{f}_{i}^{(j)}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})
is defined in Eq. (
81
).

Here we show explicitly how to compute these integrals.
Each can be reduced to the form

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\differential x\ (1-x^{2})^{\lambda-1}P_{\nu}^{\mu}(x)

(159)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi 2^{\mu}\Gamma(\lambda+\mu/2)\Gamma(\lambda-\mu/2)}{\Gamma(\lambda+\nu/2+1/2)\Gamma(\lambda-\nu/2)\Gamma(-\mu/2+\nu/2+1)\Gamma(-\mu/2-\nu/2+1/2)};

this result is given in
[
79
]
. It is valid when
2\,\text{Re}\;\lambda>|\text{Re}\;\mu|
, which will always be the case for the integrals we require in this paper.

To cast the angular integrals in the form of Eq. (
159
), in some cases we use the following linearization rule for Legendre polynomials
P_{L}
, first given in
[
80
]
, and written in a more succinct form in the Appendix of
(
81
)
:

\displaystyle P_{k}(x)P_{l}(x)=\sum_{m=|k-l|}^{k+l}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix k & l & m \\ 0 & 0 & 0\endlx@physics@matrix)^{2}(2m+1)P_{m}(x).

(160)

C.2
Specific Results

Here we present explicit results for each coefficient
C
as defined by Eq. (
158
). In the next subsection we will demonstrate how these results were calculated.

\displaystyle C_{(1)\alpha}^{(1)\beta}=C_{(2)\alpha}^{(1)\beta}=\int\differential\Omega_{q}\ Y_{\alpha}^{\beta}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})\cos\theta\cos\phi

(161)

\displaystyle=-\pi\sum_{\gamma=|\alpha-1|}^{\alpha+1}\Bigg(\sqrt{\frac{(2\gamma+1)}{3}\frac{(\gamma+1)!}{(\gamma-1)!}}\mathcal{G}_{\alpha 1\gamma}^{-101}\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta-1}\frac{\pi\Gamma(1/2)\Gamma(3/2)}{2\Gamma(3/2+\gamma/2)\Gamma(1-\gamma/2)\Gamma(3/2+\gamma/2)\Gamma(1-\gamma/2)}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\frac{(2\gamma+1)}{3}\frac{(\gamma-1)!}{(\gamma+1)!}}\mathcal{G}_{\alpha 1\gamma}^{10-1}\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta 1}\frac{2\pi\Gamma(3/2)\Gamma(1/2)}{\Gamma(3/2+\gamma/2)\Gamma(1-\gamma/2)\Gamma(1/2+\gamma/2)\Gamma(-\gamma/2)}\Bigg),

\displaystyle C_{(1)\alpha}^{(2)\beta}=C_{(2)\alpha}^{(2)\beta}=\int\differential\Omega_{q}\ Y_{\alpha}^{\beta}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})\cos\theta\sin\phi

(162)

\displaystyle=-i\pi\sum_{\gamma=|\alpha-1|}^{\alpha+1}\Bigg(\sqrt{\frac{(2\gamma+1)}{3}\frac{(\gamma+1)!}{(\gamma-1)!}}\mathcal{G}_{\alpha 1\gamma}^{-101}\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta-1}\frac{-\pi\Gamma(1/2)\Gamma(3/2)}{2\Gamma(3/2+\gamma/2)\Gamma(1-\gamma/2)\Gamma(3/2+\gamma/2)\Gamma(1-\gamma/2)}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\frac{(2\gamma+1)}{3}\frac{(\gamma-1)!}{(\gamma+1)!}}\mathcal{G}_{\alpha 1\gamma}^{10-1}\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta 1}\frac{2\pi\Gamma(3/2)\Gamma(1/2)}{\Gamma(3/2+\gamma/2)\Gamma(1-\gamma/2)\Gamma(1/2+\gamma/2)\Gamma(-\gamma/2)}\Bigg),

\displaystyle C_{(1)\alpha}^{(3)\beta}=C_{(2)\alpha}^{(3)\beta}=\int\differential\Omega_{q}\ Y_{\alpha}^{\beta}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})\cos\phi

(163)

\displaystyle=\pi\left(\sqrt{\frac{(2\alpha+1)}{4\pi}\frac{(\alpha+1)!}{(\alpha-1)!}}\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta-1}\frac{\pi\Gamma(1/2)\Gamma(3/2)}{2\Gamma(3/2+\alpha/2)\Gamma(1-\alpha/2)\Gamma(3/2+\alpha/2)\Gamma(1-\alpha/2)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\sqrt{\frac{(2\alpha+1)}{4\pi}\frac{(\alpha-1)!}{(\alpha+1)!}}\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta 1}\frac{2\pi\Gamma(3/2)\Gamma(1/2)}{\Gamma(3/2+\alpha/2)\Gamma(1-\alpha/2)\Gamma(1/2+\alpha/2)\Gamma(-\alpha/2)}\right),

\displaystyle C_{(1)\alpha}^{(4)\beta}=C_{(2)\alpha}^{(4)\beta}=\int\differential\Omega_{q}\ Y_{\alpha}^{\beta}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})\sin\phi

(164)

\displaystyle=-i\pi\left(\sqrt{\frac{(2\alpha+1)}{4\pi}\frac{(\alpha+1)!}{(\alpha-1)!}}\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta-1}\frac{\pi\Gamma(1/2)\Gamma(3/2)}{2\Gamma(3/2+\alpha/2)\Gamma(1-\alpha/2)\Gamma(3/2+\alpha/2)\Gamma(1-\alpha/2)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\sqrt{\frac{(2\alpha+1)}{4\pi}\frac{(\alpha-1)!}{(\alpha+1)!}}\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta 1}\frac{2\pi\Gamma(3/2)\Gamma(1/2)}{\Gamma(3/2+\alpha/2)\Gamma(1-\alpha/2)\Gamma(1/2+\alpha/2)\Gamma(-\alpha/2)}\right),

\displaystyle C_{(1)\alpha}^{(5)\beta}=C_{(2)\alpha}^{(5)\beta}=\int\differential\Omega_{q}\ Y_{\alpha}^{\beta}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})\sin\theta

(165)

\displaystyle=2\pi\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta 0}\left(\sqrt{\frac{(2\alpha+1)}{4\pi}}\frac{\pi\Gamma(3/2)\Gamma(3/2)}{\Gamma(2+\alpha/2)\Gamma(1+\alpha/2)\Gamma(3/2-\alpha/2)\Gamma(1/2-\alpha/2)}\right),

\displaystyle C_{(1)\alpha}^{(6)\beta}=C_{(2)\alpha}^{(7)\beta}=\int\differential\Omega_{q}\ Y_{\alpha}^{\beta}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})\;\frac{1}{\sin\theta}

(166)

\displaystyle=2\pi^{2}\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta 0}\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha+1}{4\pi}}\frac{\Gamma(3/2)\Gamma(3/2)}{\Gamma(2+\alpha/2)\Gamma(3/2-\alpha/2)\Gamma(\alpha/2+1)\Gamma(1/2-\alpha/2)},

\displaystyle C_{(1)\alpha}^{(7)\beta}=C_{(2)\alpha}^{(6)\beta}=\int\differential\Omega_{q}\ Y_{\alpha}^{\beta}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})\;\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}

(167)

\displaystyle=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha+1}{4\pi}}\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta 0}\sum_{\gamma=|\alpha-1|}^{\alpha+1}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\alpha& 1 & \gamma\\ 0 & 0 & 0\endlx@physics@matrix)^{2}(2\gamma+1)\frac{\pi\Gamma(1/2)\Gamma(1/2)}{\Gamma(1+\gamma/2)\Gamma(1/2-\gamma/2)\Gamma(1+\gamma/2)\Gamma(1/2-\gamma/2)}.

C.3
Inverse Fourier Transform Angular Integrals

We now demonstrate how to perform the angular integrals associated with the inverse FTs of §
6
. We will show explicitly how to get the results (
161
) and (
167
). We have chosen to explicitly calculate only these two results as (
161
) contains a trigonometric function in
\theta
and
\phi
in the numerator and (
167
) contains trigonometric function in the denominator. The other integrals can be computed in a very similar fashion as these two examples. We begin by computing integral (
161
):

\displaystyle C_{(1)\alpha}^{(1)\beta}=C_{(2)\alpha}^{(1)\beta}=\int\differential\Omega_{q}\ Y_{\alpha}^{\beta}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})\cos\theta\cos\phi.

(168)

Inserting the definition of the spherical harmonics and rewriting
\cos\phi
in terms of complex exponentials, we have

\displaystyle C_{(1)\alpha}^{(1)\beta}=-\sqrt{\frac{(2\alpha+1)(\alpha-\beta)!}{4\pi(\alpha+\beta)!}}\int_{1}^{-1}\differential(\cos\theta)P_{\alpha}^{\beta}(\cos\theta)P_{1}^{0}(\cos\theta)\int_{0}^{2\pi}\differential\phi\ \frac{e^{i\beta\phi}(e^{i\phi}+e^{-i\phi})}{2}

(169)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{(2\alpha+1)(\alpha-\beta)!}{4\pi(\alpha+\beta)!}}\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\delta=-\gamma}^{\gamma}(-1)^{\delta}\sqrt{\frac{4\pi(2\gamma+1)}{(2\alpha+1)(2+1)}\frac{(\alpha+\beta)!}{(\alpha-\beta)!}\frac{(\gamma-\delta)!}{(\gamma+\delta)!}}\mathcal{G}_{\alpha 1\gamma}^{\beta 0-\delta}\pi(\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta 1}+\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta-1})

\displaystyle\times\int_{-1}^{1}\differential x\;P_{\gamma}^{\delta}(x)

\displaystyle=\pi(\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta 1}+\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta-1})\sum_{\gamma=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\delta=-\gamma}^{\gamma}(-1)^{\delta}\sqrt{\frac{(2\gamma+1)}{3}\frac{(\gamma-\delta)!}{(\gamma+\delta)!}}\mathcal{G}_{\alpha 1\gamma}^{\beta 0-\delta}

\displaystyle\times\frac{\pi 2^{\delta}\Gamma(1+\delta/2)\Gamma(1-\delta/2)}{\Gamma(1+\gamma/2+1/2)\Gamma(1-\gamma/2)\Gamma(-\delta/2+\gamma/2+1)\Gamma(-\delta/2-\gamma/2+1/2)}

\displaystyle=-\pi\sum_{\gamma=|\alpha-1|}^{\alpha+1}\Bigg(\sqrt{\frac{(2\gamma+1)}{3}\frac{(\gamma+1)!}{(\gamma-1)!}}\mathcal{G}_{\alpha 1\gamma}^{-101}\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta-1}\frac{\pi\Gamma(1/2)\Gamma(3/2)}{2\Gamma(3/2+\gamma/2)\Gamma(1-\gamma/2)\Gamma(3/2+\gamma/2)\Gamma(1-\gamma/2)}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\frac{(2\gamma+1)}{3}\frac{(\gamma-1)!}{(\gamma+1)!}}\mathcal{G}_{\alpha 1\gamma}^{10-1}\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta 1}\frac{2\pi\Gamma(3/2)\Gamma(1/2)}{\Gamma(3/2+\gamma/2)\Gamma(1-\gamma/2)\Gamma(1/2+\gamma/2)\Gamma(-\gamma/2)}\Bigg).

To obtain the second equality we linearized the two associated Legendre polynomials via Eq. (
138
), and to obtain the third equality, we used Eq. (
159
).

We now turn to computing the integral (
167
). We have

\displaystyle C_{\alpha(1)}^{\beta(7)}=C_{\alpha(2)}^{\beta(6)}=\int\differential\Omega\ Y_{\alpha}^{\beta}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}

(170)

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\frac{(2\alpha+1)(\alpha-\beta)!}{4\pi(\alpha+\beta)!}}\int_{1}^{-1}\differential(\cos\theta)P_{\alpha}^{\beta}(\cos\theta)\frac{P_{1}^{0}(\cos\theta)}{\sqrt{1-\cos^{2}\theta}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\differential\varphi\ e^{i\beta\varphi}

\displaystyle=-2\pi\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha+1}{4\pi}}\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta 0}\int_{1}^{-1}\differential\mu\ P_{\alpha}(\mu)\frac{P_{1}(\mu)}{\sqrt{1-\mu^{2}}}

\displaystyle=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha+1}{4\pi}}\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta 0}\sum_{\gamma=|\alpha-1|}^{\alpha+1}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\alpha& 1 & \gamma\\ 0 & 0 & 0\endlx@physics@matrix)^{2}(2\gamma+1)\int_{-1}^{1}\differential x\ \frac{P_{\gamma}(x)}{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}

\displaystyle=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha+1}{4\pi}}\delta^{\rm K}_{\beta 0}\sum_{\gamma=|\alpha-1|}^{\alpha+1}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\alpha& 1 & \gamma\\ 0 & 0 & 0\endlx@physics@matrix)^{2}(2\gamma+1)\frac{\pi\Gamma(1/2)\Gamma(1/2)}{\Gamma(1+\gamma/2)\Gamma(1/2-\gamma/2)\Gamma(1+\gamma/2)\Gamma(1/2-\gamma/2)},

where we have used the linearization rule for a product of Legendre polynomials given in Eq. (
160
), and we have again used Eq. (
159
) to obtain the last line above.

Appendix D
Validity of the Mode Function Approximation

Throughout this work, we use Eq. (
13
) to approximate the gauge field mode functions
A_{-}(\tau,k)
, which is valid in the region
1/(8\xi)\leq-k\tau\leq 2\xi
. In the method we here present, one must frequently integrate these mode functions with respect to momentum and conformal time, during which the quantity
k\tau
falls out of the region of good approximation. In this section we check the accuracy of this approximation by computing the integrals of §
7
numerically. We must ensure that widening the bounds of integration of these integrals outside of the region of good approximation does not significantly alter their result. We begin with the integral given in Eq. (
109
),

\displaystyle M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)\equiv\int_{z_{\rm min}}^{z_{\rm max}}\differential z\,z^{2}\mathcal{A}(z)j_{a^{\prime}}(z\eta),

where
\eta\equiv r/\tau^{\prime}\leq 0
. We will have two versions of this function, one for the restricted integral
M^{R}_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
with
z_{\rm min}=-2\xi
and
z_{\rm max}=-1/(8\xi)
, and one for the full integration region of
z_{\rm min}=-2\xi
and
z_{\rm max}=-1/(8\xi)
. We experimented with different bounds for the full integral, which we denote
M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
, and found that the difference in results is negligible if the bounds are widened any further than
z_{\rm min}=-10
and
z_{\rm max}=-0.01
.

These integrals suffer from extreme oscillations due to the increasing frequency of the Bessel functions for smaller values of
\eta
. We apply smoothing

\displaystyle M^{S}_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)\equiv e^{-(\eta/\eta_{0})^{2}}M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)

(171)

and

\displaystyle M^{R,S}_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)\equiv e^{-(\eta/\eta_{0})^{2}}M^{R}_{a^{\prime}}(\eta).

(172)

We have experimented with different values of the smoothing cutoff scale
\eta_{0}
and found
\eta_{0}=3\xi
to be a good heuristic. In Figures
9
-
11
, we show that this choice of cutoff does not significantly alter either
M^{R}_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
or
M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
.

The next integral in Eq. (
111
), also contains a gauge field mode function:

\displaystyle d_{a^{\prime}}(u,v)=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\differential w\;\mathcal{S}(w)\mathcal{A}(uw)M_{a^{\prime}}(v/w).

We again want to compute the restricted and full integrals. We use the following change of variables to facilitate efficient numerical integration:
h=uv
,
\eta^{\prime}=1/u
, and
y=uw
. Thus we have

\displaystyle d_{a^{\prime}}(\eta^{\prime},h)=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\differential y\;\mathcal{S}(y\eta^{\prime})\mathcal{A}(y)M^{S}_{a^{\prime}}(h/y)

and

\displaystyle d_{a^{\prime}}^{R}(\eta^{\prime},h)=\int_{-2\xi}^{(-8\xi)^{-1}}\differential y\;\mathcal{S}(y\eta^{\prime})\mathcal{A}(y)M^{R,S}_{a^{\prime}}(h/y).

We find that the result of
d(\eta^{\prime},h)
is unchanged when the bounds are widened further than
y_{\rm min}=-10
and
y_{\rm max}=-0.001
. We again apply a smoothing to these integrals with the same cutoff value of
h_{0}=3\xi
:

\displaystyle d_{a^{\prime}}^{S}(\eta^{\prime},h)\equiv e^{-(h/h_{0})^{2}}d_{a^{\prime}}(\eta^{\prime},h),

(173)

and

\displaystyle d_{a^{\prime}}^{R,S}(\eta^{\prime},h)\equiv e^{-(h/h_{0})^{2}}d^{R}_{a^{\prime}}(\eta^{\prime},h).

(174)

In Figures
13
and
14
, we see that this choice of cutoff does not significantly alter our results.
Finally, we compute both cases of the leftmost integral in Eq. (
114
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{caa^{\prime}}(u)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential h\;h^{2}j_{c}(h\eta^{\prime})j_{a}(h)d^{S}_{a^{\prime}}(\eta^{\prime},h),

(175)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{R}_{caa^{\prime}}(u)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential h\;h^{2}j_{c}(h\eta^{\prime})j_{a}(h)d^{R,S}_{a^{\prime}}(\eta^{\prime},h),

(176)

where we have used our change of variables, and removed a factor of
u^{-4}
that can be pulled out of the integral. We can see from Figure
12
that
h^{3}d^{S}_{a^{\prime}}(\eta^{\prime},h)
and
h^{3}d^{R,S}_{a^{\prime}}(\eta^{\prime},h)
are in excellent agreement (the extra factor of
h
appears in the integrand of Eqs. (
176
) and (
176
) if one integrates over
\ln h
). Thus we have found that Eq. (
175
) will be a very good approximation for Eq. (
176
) . This shows that extending our bounds of integration outside of the region
1/(8\xi)\leq-k\tau\leq 2\xi
will not significantly affect our result.

Figure 6:
This plot shows how the function
M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
of Eq. (
D
) varies with parameter
\eta
for both the full and restricted integrals with spherical Bessel function order
a^{\prime}=0
. In our trispectrum computation, we must integrate the gauge field mode functions from
z_{\rm min}=-\infty
to
z_{\rm max}=0
; however we must truncate the integration range to some finite value to do the integrals numerically. For the full integral we integrate from
z_{\rm min}=-10
to
z_{\rm max}=-0.01
, and we have verified that widening these bounds further does not affect the integration result. For the restricted case, we do the same integration from
z_{\rm min}=-2\xi
to
z_{\rm max}=-(8\xi)^{-1}
. Throughout this paper, we use the approximation for the mode functions (
13
), which is valid only in the restricted integration range. From the figure we verify that widening our integration bounds outside of the region of good approximation does not significantly change the result.

Figure 7:
Similarly to Figure
6
, this plot verifies that the function
M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
, with spherical Bessel order
a^{\prime}=1
, does not change significantly when we integrate Eq. (
D
) over the full range (
z_{\rm min}=-10
to
z_{\rm max}=-0.01
) or the restricted range (
z_{\rm min}=-2\xi
to
z_{\rm max}=-(8\xi)^{-1}
).

Figure 8:
Similarly to Figure
6
, this plot verifies that the function
M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
, with spherical Bessel order
a^{\prime}=2
, does not change significantly when we integrate Eq. (
D
) over the full range (
z_{\rm min}=-10
to
z_{\rm max}=-0.01
) or the restricted range (
z_{\rm min}=-2\xi
to
z_{\rm max}=-(8\xi)^{-1}
).

Figure 9:
This figure shows the ratio of the smoothed to unsmoothed function,
M^{S}_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
/
M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
, as well as the normalized integral
M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
, for spherical Bessel order
a^{\prime}=0
. Since we are integrating Eq. (
D
) numerically, we must truncate the infinite integration region at some point. This truncation removes high-frequency information coming from the extreme oscillations of the spherical Bessel function at large negative values of
\eta
. This leads to a ringing behavior known as the Gibbs phenomenon. We remove the effects of the Gibbs phenomenon by applying a smoothing to the function
M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
. The vertical black lines are at
\eta=-4
and
\eta=-6
. These locations correspond respectively to
\sim
20% and
\sim
40% error from applying the smoothing for the
\xi=3
case. We can see that the smoothing only begins to significantly alter the function for values of eta where
M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
is a small fraction of its maximum value. This gives us confidence that the smoothing is not significantly affecting our result.

Figure 10:
Similarly to Figure
9
, this figure shows the ratio of the smoothed to unsmoothed function
M^{S}_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
/
M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
as well as the normalized integral
M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
for spherical Bessel order
a^{\prime}=1
. The smoothing only begins to significantly alter the function for values of eta where
M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
is a small fraction of its maximum value. We can therefore conclude that the smoothing is not significantly affecting our result.

Figure 11:
Similarly to Figure
9
, this figure shows the ratio of the smoothed to unsmoothed function
M^{S}_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
/
M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
as well as the normalized integral
M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
for spherical Bessel order
a^{\prime}=2
. The smoothing only begins to significantly alter the function for values of eta where
M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
is a small fraction of its maximum value. We can therefore conclude that the smoothing is not significantly affecting our result.

Figure 12:
In these panels we compare the full and restricted functions
d(\eta^{\prime},h)
from Eq. (
D
) at three different values of
\eta^{\prime}
. We have re-scaled the function by a factor of
h^{3}
to improve visualization of it. We can see that the full and restricted forms of the integral yield results that are in good agreement. Thus we can have confidence that integrating our function over a region containing points outside of which Eq. (
13
) is a good approximation for the mode functions will not significantly change the result.

Figure 13:
This figure shows the ratio of the smoothed, to the unsmoothed function
d^{S}(\eta^{\prime},h)
/
d(\eta^{\prime},h)
, as well as a normalized plot of the function
d(\eta^{\prime},h)
for
\xi=2
and spherical Bessel order
a^{\prime}=0
. As was the case with the function
M_{a^{\prime}}(\eta)
, truncating the
d(\eta^{\prime},h)
integral in Eq. (
D
) at a finite value, leads to a ringing effect known as Gibbs Phenomenon. To remove this effect, we again apply a smoothing to our result. The plot shows that the function
d(\eta^{\prime},h)
rapidly falls to zero outside of the points
h=0
,
\eta^{\prime}=0
, and the smoothing only significantly begins to alter the function far from this point. Thus we can have confidence that our results is unchanged by applying the smoothing.

Figure 14:
Similarly to Figure
13
, this figure shows the ratio of the smoothed, to the unsmoothed restricted function
d^{R,S}(\eta^{\prime},h)
/
d^{R}(\eta^{\prime},h)
, as well as a normalized plot of the function
d^{R}(\eta^{\prime},h)
for
\xi=2
and spherical Bessel order
a^{\prime}=0
. The fact that the function
d(\eta^{\prime},h)
rapidly falls to zero outside of the points
h=0
,
\eta^{\prime}=0
, and the smoothing only significantly begins to alter the function far from this point, gives us confidence that our result is not significantly altered by the smoothing.

Appendix E
Proof that Trispectrum Cross Terms are Parity-Even

In §
4
, we state that the trispectrum cross terms

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{Q^{\text{vac}}(\tau, \vb k_i) Q^{\text{vac}}(\tau, \vb k_j) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_l) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_m)},

(177)

are parity-even, where
i,j,l,m\in\{1,2,3,4\}
. Here we prove this statement. The imaginary part of the trispectrum is its parity-odd component, so it will suffice to show that these cross terms are real. We consider the term

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{Q^{\text{vac}}(\tau, \vb k_1) Q^{\text{vac}}(\tau, \vb k_2) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_3) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_4)}

(178)

without loss of generality as the proof will be the same for any other arrangement of the
\mathbf{k}
vectors. Since the creation and annihilation operators of the gauge field and inflaton field commute, we can separate this term into two expectation values

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{Q^{\text{vac}}(\tau, \vb k_1) Q^{\text{vac}}(\tau, \vb k_2) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_3) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_4)}=

(179)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{Q^{\text{vac}}(\tau, \vb k_1) Q^{\text{vac}}(\tau, \vb k_2)}\expectationvalue{Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_3) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_4)}.

The correlation function of the
Q^{\text{vac}}
mode functions is the power spectrum of standard inflation, which is known to be parity even, so we will focus on the power spectrum of the
Q^{\text{inv}}
mode functions:

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_3) Q^{\text{inv}}(\tau, \vb k_4)}=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\differential\tau_{3}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\differential\tau_{4}\;G(\tau,\tau_{3},k_{3})\,G(\tau,\tau_{4},k_{4})\expectationvalue{J(\tau_3, \vb k_3) \, J(\tau_4, \vb k_4)}.

(180)

Inserting Eq. (
28
) into the 2-point function of the source terms
J
in Eq. (
180
), we find

\begin{split}&\expectationvalue{J(\tau_3, \vb k_3) \, J(\tau_4, \vb k_4)}=\\
&\frac{1}{(\Lambda a(\tau))^{2}}\int\int\frac{\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{3}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{4}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,q_{3}\,q_{4}\,[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{3})][\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})]\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\times A_{-}^{\prime}(\tau_{3},|\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}|)A_{-}(\tau,q_{3})A_{-}^{\prime}(\tau_{4},|\mathbf{q}_{4}-\mathbf{k}_{4}|)A_{-}(\tau,q_{4})\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\times\expectationvalue{a_-(\vb k_3 - \vb q_3) a_-(\vb q_3) a_-^\dag(\vb q_4 - \vb k_4) a_-^\dag(-\vb q_4)},\end{split}

(181)

where we have excluded the creation and annihilation operator combinations that have a vanishing vacuum expectation value, and we used the approximation that the mode functions are real as discussed in the beginning of §
4
.

As in §
5
, we proceed by considering all possible Wick contractions:

\begin{split}\expectationvalue{a_-(\vb k_3 - \vb q_3) a_-(\vb q_3) a_-^\dag(\vb q_4 - \vb k_4) a_-^\dag(-\vb q_4)}=&(2\pi)^{6}\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}+\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{3}+\mathbf{q}_{4})\\
+&(2\pi)^{6}\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{4}+\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{3}+\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}).\end{split}

(182)

After inserting Eq. (
182
) back into Eq. (
181
), we must integrate over both pairs of delta functions. Consider the integral over the first pair

\begin{split}&\expectationvalue{J(\tau_3, \vb k_3) \, J(\tau_4, \vb k_4)}^{(1)}\equiv\\
&\frac{1}{(\Lambda a(\tau))^{2}}\int\int\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{3}\,\differential[]\mathbf{q}_{4}\,q_{3}\,q_{4}\,[\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{3})][\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\widehat{\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4}})\dotproduct\bm{\hat{\epsilon}}_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}}_{4})]\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\times A_{-}^{\prime}(\tau_{3},|\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}|)A_{-}(\tau_{3},q_{3})A_{-}^{\prime}(\tau_{4},|\mathbf{q}_{4}-\mathbf{k}_{4}|)A_{-}(\tau_{4},q_{4})\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\times\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{k}_{3}-\mathbf{q}_{3}+\mathbf{k}_{4}-\mathbf{q}_{4})\delta_{\rm D}^{[3]}(\mathbf{q}_{3}+\mathbf{q}_{4}).\end{split}

(183)

From the definition of the polarization vector
\epsilon_{-}(\hat{\mathbf{q}})=(\hat{\mathbf{\theta}}-i\hat{\mathbf{\phi}})/\sqrt{2}
, it can be shown that

\displaystyle\Re{\bm{\hat\epsilon}_- (\widehat{\vb k - \vb q}) \vdot\bm{\hat\epsilon}_- (\vu q)}=\Re{\bm{\hat\epsilon}_- (-(\widehat{\vb k - \vb q})) \vdot\bm{\hat\epsilon}_- (-\vu q)}

(184)

and

\displaystyle\Im{\bm{\hat\epsilon}_- (\widehat{\vb k - \vb q}) \vdot\bm{\hat\epsilon}_- (\vu q)}=-\Im{\bm{\hat\epsilon}_- (-(\widehat{\vb k - \vb q})) \vdot\bm{\hat\epsilon}_- (-\vu q)}.

(185)

Therefore, when we multiply the two polarization vector dot products in Eq. (
183
), the two imaginary terms cancel and we are left with a real function. For precisely the same reason, the integral over the other pair of delta functions in Eq. (
182
) is also a real function. We have thus shown that the power spectrum of the
Q^{\text{inv}}
mode functions is real and therefore the trispectrum cross term given in Eq. (
178
) is parity-even.
